K13.652-653K. XIII 652–653, ch. 4.1 difficulty mid (4.83/100; thin 5, absent 2) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπεὶ δὲ τινὰ μὲν ἐκ τῶν ἐπιῤῥεόντων τοῖς ἡλκωμένοις μέρεσιν ἰχώρων τε καὶ χυμῶν δυσεπούλωτα γίγνεται, 653 τινὰ δ’ αὐτοῦ τοῦ πεπονθότος μορίου διαφθείροντος τὸ ἐπιῤῥέον, εἰ καὶ χρηστὸν εἴη, τὰ κατὰ τὸν δεύτερον τρόπον ἕλκη, δυσεπούλωτα καὶ κακοήθη καλοῦσιν ἰδίως ἐφ’ ὧν ἐνίοτε πᾶν ἐκκόπτειν τὸ πεπονθὸς ἢ καίειν ἀναγκάζονταί τινες, ἤτοι φαρμάκοις ἐσχαρωτικοῖς ἢ πυρὶ χρώμενοι. πλάτους δ’ ὄντος συχνοῦ καθ’ ἑκάτερον τὸν τρόπον ἐν τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον ἕτερον ἑτέρου δυσεπούλωτόν τε καὶ κακόηθες εἶναι, τοσοῦτον ἀριθμὸν ὑπάρχειν τῶν ἰωμένων φαρμάκων ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστιν, ὅσος ἂν ᾖ καὶ αὐτῶν τῶν ἑλκῶν. εἰκότως οὖν ἕκαστον τῶν ἕν ἐχόντων φάρμακον ἐπαγγελλόμενον ἐπουλοῦν ἕλκη κακοήθη, πολλάκις ἀποτυγχάνει. δύο γὰρ ἡτοιμάσθαι χρὴ τοὐλάχιστον, ᾧ πρόκειται πάντα θεραπεύειν τὰ τοιαῦτα, τῆς αὐτῆς μὲν ἐπαγγελίας καὶ δυνάμεως ὑπάρχοντα, διαφέροντα δὲ ἀλλήλων οὐχ ἁπλῶς τῷ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον, ἀλλ’ ὡς ἄν τις εἴποι σαφέστατα τῷ μάλιστα τε καὶ ἥκιστα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But since some ulcers become hard to cicatrize from the ichors and humours that flow in to the ulcerated parts, 653 while others do so because the affected part itself corrupts that which flows in, even if it be good, it is the ulcers of the second mode that they call, in the proper sense, hard to cicatrize and malignant; in these cases some are sometimes compelled to excise the whole affected part or to burn it, using either escharotic drugs or fire. And since in each of the two modes there is considerable latitude in respect of the more and the less, one ulcer being more hard to cicatrize and malignant than another, it is necessary that there be as great a number of the drugs that cure them as there is of the ulcers themselves. Reasonably, therefore, each of those who possess a single drug professing to cicatrize malignant ulcers often fails. For at least two must have been prepared by one whose aim is to treat all such ulcers, drugs of the same professed virtue and power, but differing from one another not simply in the more and the less, but, as one might put it most clearly, in the most and the least.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἕκαστον τῶν ἕν ἐχόντων φάρμακον ἐπαγγελλόμενον

Since some ulcers become hard to cicatrise from the ichors and humours flowing in upon the ulcerated parts, 653 and others because the affected part itself corrupts what flows in, even if it should be wholesome, the ulcers of the second kind they call specifically 'hard to cicatrise and malignant'. In these some people are sometimes compelled to excise the whole affected part or to burn it, using either escharotic drugs or fire. And since there is considerable latitude in each of the two kinds, in that one ulcer is more or less hard to cicatrise and malignant than another, the number of drugs that heal them must necessarily be as great as that of the ulcers themselves. Reasonably, then, each of those who possess a single drug that professes to cicatrise malignant ulcers often fails. For whoever proposes to treat all such ulcers must have prepared at least two drugs, of the same claim and power, but differing from one another not simply by more and less but, as one might put it most clearly, by most and least.

K13.653-654K. XIII 653–654, ch. 4.1 difficulty low (1.78/100; thin 1, absent 2) (a) pass (b) fail 5/8 (c) pass 14/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

καθάπερ οὖν ἐπὶ πάντων τῶν παθῶν ἐδείκνυμεν ἐν τοῖς τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου γράμμασιν εὑρίσκεσθαι πρώτην μὲν ἁπασῶν τὴν καθόλου δύναμιν τῶν 654 βοηθημάτων, ἐφεξῆς δὲ τὰς ὕλας, ἐν αἷς αὐτὴ περιέχεται, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν λόγον καὶ νῦν ἐπὶ τῶν δυσεπουλώτων ἑλκῶν ἡ καθόλου δύναμις ἥτις ποτέ ἐστι τῶν θεραπευσόντων αὐτὰ φαρμάκων εὑρῆσθαι χρὴ πρότερον. ἐδείχθη δέ μοι καὶ ἥδε κατ’ ἐκείνην τὴν πραγματείαν, ἐφ’ ὧν μὲν οὐδεμία καχεξία περὶ τοῖς ἡλκωμένοις μέρεσι, δύο τούτους ἔχουσα σκοποὺς, ἕνα μὲν ἀποκρούσασθαι τὸ ἐπιῤῥέον, ἕτερον δὲ διαφορῆσαι τὸ ἐστηριγμένον ἐν τῷ πεπονθότι μορίῳ. γίγνεται δὲ ταῦτα δι’ ἐναντίων δυνάμεων. ἀναστέλλει μὲν γὰρ τὸ ἐπιῤῥέον ὅσα στύφειν πέφυκε φάρμακα, διαφορεῖ δὲ τὸ περιεχόμενον ἐν τῷ πεπονθότι τὰ δύναμιν ἑλκτικὴν ἔχοντα. τοὐπίπαν δὲ τὰ μὲν στύφοντα τραχύνει καὶ κατὰ τοῦτ’ ἔστιν ἀνιαρά. τὰ δὲ ἕλκοντα καὶ διαφοροῦντα τὰς ἐκ βάθους ὑγρότητας οὐκ ἄνευ δριμύτητος ἐργάζεται τοῦτο. τῇ δριμύτητι δ’ ἐξ ἀνάγκης ὀδύνη τις ἕπεται δάκνουσα τὸ ἡλκωμένον. καὶ οὕτως συμβαίνει διὰ τὴν ἐκ τῶν τραχυνόντων καὶ δακνόντων ἀνίαν, ἐρεθίζεσθαι τὴν ἐπιῤῥέουσαν ὑγρότητα τοῖς ἡλκωμένοις μέρεσι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Just as, then, in the case of all affections we showed in the books of the Therapeutic Method that what is discovered first of all is the general power of the 654 remedies, and next the materials in which that power is contained, by the same reasoning now too, in the case of ulcers hard to cicatrize, the general power, whatever it is, of the drugs that will treat them must first be discovered. And this too was shown by me in that treatise: in those cases where there is no cachexia about the ulcerated parts, it has these two aims, one to repel that which flows in, the other to disperse what is lodged in the affected part. These things come about through opposite powers. For all drugs whose nature is to astringe drive back that which flows in, while those possessing an attractive power disperse what is contained in the affected part. For the most part, however, astringent things roughen, and in this respect they are distressing; while those that attract and disperse the moistures from the depth do not accomplish this without acridity. And upon acridity there necessarily follows a certain pain that bites the ulcerated part. And so it comes about that, because of the distress arising from the roughening and biting agents, the moisture flowing in to the ulcerated parts is provoked.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

Just as, then, in the books of the Therapeutic Method we showed that in the case of all affections what must be found first of all is the general power of the 654 remedies, and next the materials in which that power is contained, so by the same reasoning now too, in the case of sores hard to cicatrise, the general power, whatever it may be, of the drugs that are to cure them must be found first. This too was shown by me in that treatise: in cases where there is no bad condition about the ulcerated parts, it has these two aims, one to repel what flows in, the other to disperse what has become lodged in the affected part. These are accomplished through opposite powers. For drugs that are by nature astringent check what flows in, while those that have a drawing power disperse what is contained in the affected part. In general, however, astringent drugs roughen, and in this respect they are distressing. Those that draw and disperse the moistures from the depth do not accomplish this without acridity. And acridity is of necessity followed by a certain pain that bites the ulcerated part. So it comes about that, through the distress caused by the roughening and biting drugs, the moisture flowing in upon the ulcerated parts is provoked.

K13.654-655K. XIII 654–655, ch. 4.1 difficulty low (1.88/100; thin 3, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

τούτου δὲ γιγνομένου, φλεγμαίνειν 655 τε ἅμα καὶ διαβιβρώσκεσθαι τὸ ἕλκος, ὥσθ’ ὅσον ἐπὶ τοῖς εἰρημένοις ἄπορός ἐστιν ἡ τῶν βοηθησόντων φαρμάκων εὕρεσις τοῖς δυσεπουλώτοις ἕλκεσι, καὶ πολύ γε μᾶλλον ἔτι τοῖς κακοήθεσιν. εὑρεῖν οὖν χρὴ καθόσον οἷόν τε τοιαύτην ὕλην φαρμάκων, ἐφ’ ἧς ξηρανθήσεται τὸ ἕλκος ὀδύνης χωρὶς, ὅπερ ἀδύνατόν ἐστιν ἀκριβῶς γενέσθαι μὴ γνωρίζοντος τοῦ θεραπεύοντος, ὅπως ἐνεργήσῃ τὸ προβληθὲν φάρμακον. ἔστι μὲν γὰρ ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἰσχυρὸν κατ’ ἄμφω τὰ συντεθέντα τό τε δάκνον καὶ τὸ στῦφον. ἀλλ’ ὅ τε ῥύπος ὁ ἐπικείμενος τοῖς τοιούτοις ἕλκεσι τό τε τῆς ὑγρότητος πλῆθος οἷον προβλήματά τινα τοῖς ἡλκωμένοις μέρεσι γιγνόμενα τὴν ἀπὸ τῶν ἰσχυρῶν φαρμάκων δῆξιν ἐκλύει. προσέχειν οὖν χρὴ πόσον ἡ πρώτη χρῆσις ἐξήρανε τῆς κατὰ τὸ ἕλκος ὑγρότητος, ἢ εἰ καὶ ῥύπον ἔχοι, πόσον ἀπέῤῥιψε καὶ τούτου. καὶ οὕτως ἐκ δευτέρου τε καὶ τρίτου χρῆσθαι μέχρι περ ἂν φανῇ καθαρόν τε καὶ ξηρόν. ὁπόταν δὲ πρῶτον ἴδῃς αὐτὸ τοιοῦτον, μεταβαίνειν ἐφ’ ἕτερον φάρμακον ὁμοειδὲς μὲν, ἀσθενέστερον δὲ τοῦ προτέρου.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προβλήματά

And when this happens, the ulcer both becomes inflamed 655 and at the same time is eaten away, so that, as far as depends on what has been said, the discovery of drugs that will help ulcers that are hard to cicatrize is without resource, and still much more so for malignant ones. One must therefore find, as far as possible, such a material of drugs by which the ulcer will be dried without pain—something which cannot be done precisely unless the one treating knows how the drug applied will act. For it is of necessity strong in both of the components that have been combined, the biting and the astringent. But the filth lying upon such ulcers and the abundance of moisture, becoming as it were screens for the ulcerated parts, dissolve the bitingness from the strong drugs. One must therefore attend to how much of the moisture in the ulcer the first application dried up, or, if it also has filth, how much of this too it cast off; and in this way to use it a second and a third time until it appears both clean and dry. And as soon as you see it in such a state, to pass over to another drug, of the same kind but weaker than the former.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἄπορος, προβλήματα, ἐφ’ ἧς, τὰ συντεθέντα

When this happens, the ulcer becomes inflamed 655 and at the same time is eaten through, so that, as far as what has been said goes, the discovery of drugs that will help ulcers hard to cicatrise is a matter without a ready way, and much more still for malignant ones. One must therefore find, so far as is possible, a material of drugs of such a kind that by it the ulcer will be dried without pain; and this cannot be done precisely unless the one treating knows how the drug applied will act. For it is of necessity strong in both of the things combined in it, the biting and the astringent. But the filth that lies upon such ulcers and the quantity of moisture, becoming as it were a kind of protective covering for the ulcerated parts, weaken the biting that comes from the strong drugs. One must therefore attend to how much of the moisture in the ulcer the first application has dried, or, if it also has filth, how much of this too it has thrown off. And in this way one should use it a second and a third time, until the ulcer appears clean and dry. And as soon as you first see it in such a state, pass over to another drug of the same kind, but weaker than the former.

K13.655-656K. XIII 655–656, ch. 4.1 difficulty low (1.37/100; thin 0, absent 2) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 15/16 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

τὰ γὰρ ἰσχυρότερα 656 τῷ δάκνειν τὸ ἕλκος ὀδυνηρὰ γινόμενα, ῥεύματά τε κινεῖ καὶ φλεγμονὰς ἐγείρει. ἀλλ’ ἐὰν πάλιν ἀσθενέστερον πολὺ τοῦ πρώτου φαρμάκου προσενέγκῃς, ὑγρὸν καὶ ῥυπαρόν ἔσται τὸ ἕλκος. τούτῳ τοίνυν ὁ τεχνωθεὶς ἰατρὸς τοῦ διὰ μόνης ἐμπειρίας ἕλκος θεραπεύοντος ἀμείνων ἐστὶν, ὡς ἂν ἐπιστάμενος οὐκ ἄλλῃ δυνάμει θεραπεύεσθαι τὸ ἕλκος ὑπὸ τῶν φαρμάκων, ὅτι μὴ τῷ ξηρὸν οὕτως ἐργάζεσθαι τὸ μόριον, ὡς ὅτ’ εἶχε κατὰ φύσιν. ἔσται δὲ τοῦτο δῆλον ἐκ τοῦ μήτ’ ἀποῤῥεῖν ἔτι λεπτὸν ἰχῶρα πύου τε γένεσιν ὑποτρέφεσθαι. εἰς τοῦτο δὲ ἀφίξεσθαι, καθ’ ὃν εἴρηκα τρόπον ξηραινόμενα, τουτέστι στυφόμενά τε καὶ διαφορούμενα, κᾀπειδὰν ξηρὰ μὲν ᾖ, ῥυπαρὰ δ’ ἔτι φαίνηται, διὰ τῶν ῥυπτικῶν φαρμάκων καθαιρόμενα, περὶ ὧν καὶ αὐτῶν εἴρηται μέν τι κᾀν τοῖς περὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων ὑπομνήμασιν. ἡ τελειοτάτη δὲ μέθοδος πᾶσα γέγραπται διὰ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς πραγματείας, ἐν ᾗ προτετεχνῶσθαι χρὴ τὸν μέλλοντα τελέως ὑπὸ τῶν νῦν λεγομένων ὠφεληθήσεσθαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀφίξεσθαι

For the stronger ones, 656 becoming painful by biting the ulcer, both set fluxes in motion and rouse inflammations. But if, on the other hand, you apply one much weaker than the first drug, the ulcer will be moist and foul. In this, then, the physician trained in the art is better than the one who treats an ulcer through experience alone, inasmuch as he knows that the ulcer is treated by drugs through no other power than that of rendering the part as dry as it was when it was in its natural state. This will be evident from the fact that neither does thin ichor flow off any longer nor is a generation of pus fostered. And they will arrive at this by being dried in the manner I have stated, that is, by being astringed and dispersed, and, when they are dry but still appear foul, by being cleansed through detergent drugs, concerning which, too, something has been said also in the commentaries on simple drugs. But the most complete method has been written in its entirety in the Therapeutic treatise, in which the one who is going to profit fully from what is now being said must have been previously trained in the art.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μήτ’ ἀποῤῥεῖν ἔτι λεπτὸν ἰχῶρα πύου τε γένεσιν ὑποτρέφεσθαι (μήτε … τε: unclear whether the generation of pus is fostered or is also negated), ὑποτρέφεσθαι, ἀφίξεσθαι (infinitive without an expressed governing verb; subject understood as the ulcers), διαφορούμενα

For the stronger ones 656, becoming painful by biting the ulcer, both set fluxes in motion and rouse inflammations. But if, on the other hand, you apply one much weaker than the first drug, the ulcer will be moist and foul. In this respect, then, the physician trained in the art is better than the one who treats an ulcer through experience alone, inasmuch as he knows that the ulcer is healed by drugs through no other power than their making the part dry in the way it was when it was in its natural state. This will be evident from thin ichor no longer flowing off, and the generation of pus being fostered. And they will arrive at this when they are dried in the manner I have described, that is, when they are astringed and dispersed, and, once they are dry but still appear foul, when they are cleansed by means of detergent drugs. Something has also been said about these in the commentaries on simple drugs. But the most complete method has been written out in its entirety in the therapeutic treatise, in which anyone who is to be fully benefited by what is now being said must have been trained beforehand.

τοῦτο δ’ ὑμῖν τοῖς ἑταίροις ἐγνωκὸς ὑπάρχειν, οἷς ταῦτα γράφεται, πάνυ σαφῶς ﻿ 657 οἶδα καὶ πρὸς τούτῳ γε ἔτι τὸ μὴ λόγον εἶναι πιθανὸν ἄνευ τῆς ἐπὶ τῶν ἔργων βασάνου τὰ λεγόμενα νῦν ὑπ’ ἐμοῦ. πολλάκις γὰρ ἐθεάσασθε δι’ αὐτῶν ἐκείνων τῶν φαρμάκων, οἷς ἀμεθόδως χρώμενοί τινες ἰατροὶ μηδὲν ἤνυον, ἐκθεραπεύσαντά με πολλὰ τῶν τοιούτων ἑλκῶν. ἀναμνήσθητε τοίνυν τῶν ξηραίνειν ἀδήκτως πεφυκότων φαρμάκων, ἔστι δ’ αὐτῶν τὰ πλεῖστα μεταλλικά. καὶ μέντοι καυθέντων καὶ πλυθέντων, ἥτις ἦν πρόσθεν δῆξις ἀπόλλυται, καὶ τοῦτ’ ἀναμνήσθητε. τούτοις οὖν ὑμῖν ἐξέστω χρῆσθαι ἐν τῷ παρόντι, πεπλυμένοις μὲν ἐπὶ παίδων, εὐνούχων τε καὶ γυναικῶν καὶ ὅσοι φύσει λευκὴν καὶ μαλακὴν ἔχουσι τὴν σάρκα, καὶ πρὸς τούτοις γ’ ἐπὶ τῶν εὐαισθήτων τε πάνυ καὶ κακοχύμων. ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν ἄλλων σωμάτων ἐγχωρεῖ καὶ τοῖς ἀπλύτοις χρῆσθαι, τὴν πρώτην ἐπίθεσιν αὐτῶν εὐπεπτηκόσι τοῖς θεραπευομένοις ἕωθεν ποιουμένους. εἰ δὲ καὶ τοῦ σώματος ὅλου προπαρασκευτῆς τινος δέοι, καὶ ταύτην χρὴ προπεποιῆσθαι. λελεγμέναι δ’ εἰσὶν αἱ παρασκευαὶ διὰ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς πραγματείας.

And that this has been settled for you, my companions, for whom these things are written, I know very clearly, 657 and in addition to this, further, that what is now being said by me is not merely a persuasive argument lacking the testing on actual practice. For you have often seen me cure completely many such ulcers by means of those very drugs with which certain physicians, using them unmethodically, accomplished nothing. Recall, then, the drugs whose nature it is to dry without biting; most of them are metallic substances. And indeed, when they have been burnt and washed, whatever bitingness there was before is lost—recall this too. So let it be permitted to you to use these in the present case: washed, in the case of children, eunuchs and women and all who by nature have white and soft flesh, and in addition to these in the case of those who are very sensitive and full of bad humours. But in the case of other bodies it is possible to use even the unwashed ones, making the first application of them in the morning, when those being treated have digested well. And if the whole body should also need some preliminary preparation, this too must have been carried out beforehand. The preparations have been described in the therapeutic treatise.

In other bodies one may also use the unwashed ones, making the first application of them early in the morning, when the patients under treatment have digested well. And if some preparation of the whole body beforehand is needed, this too must have been carried out first. The preparations have been described in the therapeutic treatise.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τάξεως

For in some cases it suffices merely to purge, 658 in some to remove blood, and in others to do both of these; in some, to cut out a varix lying above the ulcer, or to take care of the spleen or the liver; and in some also to scarify the parts around the ulcer, just as sometimes also to cut away the callus of the lips. But if we need none of these, one must examine the quantity of the moisture together with the filth, and so proceed to the appropriate drug, having recalled, among each of the simples, which ones dry but do not cleanse away the filth of the ulcer, which ones cleanse but do not dry vigorously, and which ones are of such a nature as to do both, considering in addition of what order each one is—a matter which is the most important of those that bear upon the treatment, yet has been neglected by those who wrote the books on materia medica. For these alone undertook to state the powers of the whole of it, as indeed did Dioscorides, whom one would say is second to none of those who have devoted their efforts to these matters. I, therefore, shall enumerate in order all the drugs that are able to dry ulcers without biting or roughening noticeably.

I shall begin 659 with the metallic substances, among which they also count the kinds of earth. Since there is much difference among them in respect of drying more and less, I shall begin from the intermediate ones and pass on to those which dry more strongly or more feebly. Moderately drying, then, are the Lemnian seal and cadmia, and more so than the latter the naturally occurring kind, which, without undergoing any transformation, is found in the winter torrents and streams and in the mountains of the Cyprians, and in other regions too, but especially in those where the mines are. I am accustomed to prepare it so as to be more drying, in the season of summer, by grinding it in the sun for several days in succession with vinegar or with wine that is tawny in colour and translucent in consistency, such as the Falernian is in Italy and the Tmolite in Asia; and such a wine is the most drying of wines. Of the same class are the Ariusian, produced in Chios, and the fragrant Lesbian at Eresos and Methymna.

I shall begin 659 with the metallic substances, among which they also count the kinds of earth. Since there is great difference among them in drying more and less, I shall begin with those in the middle and pass on to those that dry more vehemently or more feebly. Now, Lemnian seal dries moderately, and so does cadmia; and more than this the natural [cadmia], which has not undergone transformation and is found in torrents and streams, in the mountains of the Cypriots, and in other places, but especially in those where there are mines. I am accustomed to make it more drying by grinding it in summertime in the sun for many days in succession with vinegar or with wine that is tawny in colour but clear in consistency, such as Falernian in Italy and Tmolite in Asia; wine of this sort is the most drying of wines. Of the same kind are the Ariusian produced in Chios and the fragrant Lesbian of Eresus and Methymna.

τούτων δ’ ἑξῆς εἰσιν οἱ παλαιοὶ πάντες, ὅταν ἐκ τῶν λευκῶν καὶ στυφόντων παλαιωθῶσιν, ἐν Ἰταλίᾳ μὲν ὅ τε Σιγνῖνος καὶ ὁ Τιβουρτῖνος καὶ Μαρσὸς, ἐν Σικελίᾳ δὲ ὁ καλούμενος ὑπ’ αὐτῶν Ἀμιναῖος, ἐν Ἀσία δὲ ὁ τ’ Ἀφροδισιεὺς 660 καὶ ὁ Μίσυος. ἀρκεῖ γὰρ ὡς παραδείγματα τῶν καθόλου δυνάμεων εἰρῆσθαί μοι τούτους. ἀλλὰ καὶ δι’ ὄξους δριμέος ὁμοίως ἐν ἡλίῳ πολλαῖς ἡμέραις ὥρᾳ θέρους παρασκευάζω τὴν καδμείαν. ἀποτίθεμαί τε ξηράνας, ὡς ἐπιπάττειν δύνασθαι τοῖς κακοηθευομένοις ἕλκεσιν. ἰσχυρότερον δὲ τούτου φάρμακόν ἐστι χαλκῖτις κεκαυμένη προλειωθεῖσα τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον τῇ καδμείᾳ, μετ’ αὐτὴν δ’ ἄκαυστος ἰσχυρὸν οὕτως ἐστὶ φάρμακον ὡς ἐσχάρας ἐργάζεσθαι. πεπλυμένην οὖν αὐτὴν, εἶτα ἐξηρασμένην ἔχειν ἄμεινον. ἀσθενέστερον μὲν γὰρ τῆς κεκαυμένης ἡ πεπλυμένη ξηραίνει, πρᾳοτέρα δέ ἐστι πολὺ καὶ ἀδηκτοτέρα. παραπλήσιον δὲ αὐτῇ φάρμακόν ἐστι καὶ ὁ κεκαυμένος ἰός· ὁ δὲ ἄκαυστος ἰσχυρότατον φάρμακον, ὥσπερ καὶ ἡ χαλκῖτις. ἀλλ’ ἐκείνῃ μὲν καὶ στύψεώς τι μέτεστι καὶ πολὺ μᾶλλον αὐτῆς τῷ χαλκάνθῳ, ξηραντικωτάτῳ τῶν μεταλλικῶν ὄντι. τῷ δὲ ἰῷ τοιαύτης δυνάμεως οὐδ’ ὅλως μέτεστιν. ὁμογενὲς δ’ ὡς ἐμάθετε τῇ χαλκίτιδι τό τε μίσυ καὶ τὸ σῶρυ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: Μίσυος

Next after these come all the old wines, whenever they have been aged from among the white and astringent ones: in Italy the Signine, the Tiburtine and the Marsian; in Sicily the one called by them Aminaean; in Asia the Aphrodisian 660 and the Mysian (Μίσυος). For it suffices for me to have mentioned these as examples of the general powers. But I also prepare the cadmia in like manner with acrid vinegar, in the sun for many days in the summer season. And having dried it I store it away, so as to be able to sprinkle it on ulcers that are turning malignant. A stronger drug than this is burnt chalcitis, previously levigated in the same manner as the cadmia; after it the unburnt is so strong a drug as to produce eschars. It is better, therefore, to have it washed and then dried. For the washed dries more weakly than the burnt, but it is much milder and less biting. Burnt verdigris, too, is a drug similar to it; but the unburnt is a very strong drug, just as chalcitis is. But the latter has some share also of astringency, and much more than it has chalcanthum, which is the most drying of the metallic substances; whereas verdigris has no share at all of such a power. And of the same kind as chalcitis, as you have learned, are misy and sory.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Μίσυος, μετ’ αὐτὴν δ’ ἄκαυστος, κακοηθευομένοις

Next after these come all the old wines, when they have aged from white and astringent ones: in Italy the Signine, the Tiburtine and the Marsian; in Sicily the one they call Aminaean; in Asia the Aphrodisian 660 and the Misyan. It is enough for me to have named these as examples of the general powers. I also prepare cadmia in the same way with acrid vinegar, in the sun for many days in the summer season. Having dried it I store it, so as to be able to sprinkle it on ulcers that are turning malignant. A stronger drug than this is burnt chalcitis, first ground fine in the same manner as the cadmia; next after it, the unburnt chalcitis is so strong a drug as to produce eschars. It is therefore better to keep it washed and then dried. For the washed dries more weakly than the burnt, but it is much milder and less biting. A drug similar to it is burnt verdigris; the unburnt verdigris is a very strong drug, as is chalcitis too. But chalcitis has some share of astringency, and chalcanthum has much more of it than chalcitis does, being the most drying of the metallic substances. Verdigris, however, has no share at all of such a power. Of the same kind as chalcitis, as you have learned, are misy and sory.

For they are, as one might say, sprung from a single root, misy being finer in its parts 661 and less biting and caustic, while sory is thicker in its parts and less drying than both, just as stimmi too has a power more astringent and more drying than sory. A most suitable drug for ulcers that are hard to cicatrize because of moisture is also the one called diphryges, for it dries strongly, although it bites only moderately; and it too has, in addition to the acrid quality and power, the astringent one, both of which raw chalcitis and chalcanthum have in stronger measure—whether one wishes to name it in the masculine, or in the feminine, or in the neuter. For always remember not to quarrel over names where the task before us is to learn thoroughly a theory necessary for the works of the art. And all the kinds of alum too dry ulcers sufficiently, being vehemently astringent. Hence one would not use them on their own upon an ulcer, just as one would not use raw chalcitis and verdigris either. But when these and misy have been burnt they are useful, as indeed also when they have been washed: they become non-biting, but dry less.

Hence one would not use them alone on a sore, just as one would not use raw chalcitis or verdigris alone either. It is safer, then, to use them washed, since most of the bodies which we are compelled to treat 662 have at least some one disposition that does not bear strong bitingness. You have heard these things often, but now too I shall remind you of them again, and I shall ask you to keep them in mind throughout the whole discourse that follows. Accordingly, all bodies that are plethoric and all that are full of bad humours, and in addition to these those that are soft-fleshed and considerably sensitive, are easily brought into inflammation when bitten. Included among the soft-fleshed are, evidently, the bodies of white women and those of children, eunuchs, and men white in complexion; and of those who inhabit a cold land, too, the bodies are moist and phlegmatic and for this reason both soft and white, just as those who inhabit a hot land, such as those in Egypt and Arabia, have a hard and dry habit of body. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πυρῶδες, θερμαντηρίοις

But the best drug for such cases is also burnt lead, 663 and after it sory and stimmi, these too burnt. And the dross of lead dries well, as does also the scale of iron, and still more that of steel (stomōma). But that of copper, in addition to drying, also cleanses, wherefore it also makes the ulcers clean, the other scales not having this property. Sprinkling, then, all these drugs dry upon malignant ulcers, you will see them wonderfully dried without biting, as also the burnt shells of whelks, of purple-shells and of oysters, and all other such things. And the so-called transparent stone, which the Romans name specularium, is, when burnt, among the things that dry without biting, being useful for ulcers that are hard to cicatrize, though not for malignant ones; for it dries more moderately than is required for these. Of the same power as it are also burnt glass and the fiery deposit that forms in the copper heating-vessels.

An excellent drug for such conditions is also burnt lead, 663 and after it sory and stimmi, these too burnt. The slag of lead also dries well, just as the flake of iron does too, and still more that of steel. That of copper, besides drying, also cleanses, which is why it also makes ulcers clean, the other flakes not having this property. So if you sprinkle all these drugs dry on malignant ulcers, you will see the ulcers dried wonderfully without biting. The same is true of the burnt shells of trumpet-shells, of purple-shells and of oysters, and of all other such things. The so-called transparent stone, which the Romans name specularium, is also among the things that dry without biting, when burnt. It is useful for ulcers that are hard to cicatrise, but not for malignant ones, for it dries more moderately than these require. Of the same power as it are also burnt glass and the fiery matter that forms in bronze heating-vessels.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τοῦ παχυμερεστέρων, ἡ ἄσβεστος

Along with drying, the fruit of the Egyptian thorn (akantha) has in addition also an astringent power, as do the omphakitis gall-nut and rheon and pomegranate rinds, and not a few 664 roots and herbs. For indeed aristolochia and iris and akoron and artemisia and the root of panax and argemone, and the root of black chamaeleon and kyperon, and drakontion and xiphion dry sufficiently, and still more than these do kentaurion and polion. Slightly weaker than these is the earth called Eretrian, just as the ampelitis is stronger. Such, then, is one body of material of the simple drugs that treat malignant ulcers. From outside there are added to these certain things which are not applied by themselves, because of the vehemence of their power, but are mixed with others, such as unburnt verdigris (ios) and quicklime (asbestos) and unwashed lime (titanos) and soft aphronitron, which is in truth aphrolitron. For the more foamy it is, the better it is in both respects, both in biting less and in dispersing more. For you learned also in the books on simple drugs that the finer-parted substances possess such a power in comparison with the coarser-parted ones, among all things of the same kind.

For you learned also in the works on simple drugs that, among all things of the same kind, the finer-particled have a power of this sort in comparison with the coarser-particled.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πλάτυσμα, κάγχρυ, ἴσον ἁπάντῃ

But nothing is so much to be guarded against as the drugs called by Hippocrates 'softening' (malthakōdē), which belong to the 665 genus of oil. Of this kind are all the fats, and above all among them pig's fat, then next the resins, and after these wax. In another way, the emollients of hardened parts are also unsuitable: galbanum, bdellium, ammoniac incense, storax and the marrows. And in yet another way the acrid ones: pepper, ginger, mustard, kanchry and a great multitude of other such things. [How one should use the dry drugs just mentioned in the case of ulcers that are difficult to cicatrize and malignant.] It is necessary that such ulcers be full of bad moisture, and not a few of them also have abundant filth. Whenever, therefore, we are about to sprinkle on them one of the dry drugs, one must first wipe them out with a linen cloth that is soft, clean and dry, and, having filled the hollow of the ulcer with the drug, apply on the outside a flat piece (platysma) of a plaster that thins the lips, and over this again, on the outside, a linen cloth equal on every side, and over it a sponge from water or wine.

One must make it a matter of the utmost concern that the 666 sponge remain cold, which would easily come about if one continually poured cold water over it, without undoing the bandage. The bandage itself should be applied and wound round as Hippocrates taught in the books On Fractures, holding that the greatest harm would result from a bandaging that excels in fine appearance rather than in benefit. If, then, the bandage is a single one, it should proceed from below upwards, whether it be the shin, or the knee, or the ankle, or the elbow or the hand at its extremity, or any other part whatsoever (ἠέρος). But if you wish to use two, as in the case of fractures, let the first proceed from the affected part towards the region above only, and let the second go first from below, and then successively upwards, ending so as to arrive at the same place as the first. It is clear, then, that these bandages should be made of linen cloths, not of rag. One should undo them every third day, not every day, and having undone them not apply water to the ulcer, but, in the manner just described, wipe away the ichors with a linen cloth that is soft, clean and dry.

One must make it of the utmost importance that the 666 sponge remains cold, which could easily be done if one continually pours cold water over it, without undoing the bandage. The bandage itself should be applied and wound round as Hippocrates taught in his works on fractures, since he held that the greatest harm would come from a bandaging that tends towards neat appearance and not towards benefit. If, then, there is a single bandage, it must be carried from below upwards, whether it is the shin, or the knee, or the ankle, or the elbow or the extremity of the hand, or any other part whatever. But if you wish to use two, as in the case of fractures, let the first be carried from the affected part towards the upper region only, and let the second be carried first from below, and next, ending above, let it come up to that very point to join the first. It is therefore evident that these bandages ought to be made of strips of linen, not of rag. One should undo them every third day, not every day, and, having undone them, should not apply water to the ulcer, but, in the manner described just now, wipe off the ichors with a soft, clean and dry linen cloth.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πρόσθεν, ἀσφαλέστερον δὲ, κᾂν

But if the parts around the ulcer also display something inflammatory, apply over the flat piece (platysma), from outside, extending over a wide area, 667 a liquid drug made from my phoinikine, not simply melted, but having taken up as much astringent wine as possible. Nor indeed is it proper to melt it in any oil whatever; rather, if some redness, or in general heat, prevails in the parts around the ulcer, in one of the astringent and cooling oils—such are myrtle oil and quince oil; and if these are not at hand, the oil called omphakine and omotribes (of unripe olives); but if hardness appears to prevail, the same phoinikine must be melted in old oil or in castor oil (kikinon). And it is safer, even if nothing bad appears in the region surrounding the ulcer, at any rate to apply the liquid drug to the region itself lying above it, so that it may repel whatever, from before (πρόσθεν), either bad or more than is proper, flows in, and especially when the ulcer, being on the leg, has a varicose vein lying above it. And if this is large and shows by its colour that it contains blood that is neither red nor good, but blacker and more melancholic, one must cut out the vein.

If the parts around the ulcer also show something phlegmonous, one should apply over the flat spread, on the outside, extending over a wide area 667, a liquid drug made from my palm plaster (phoinikinē), not simply melted, but having taken up as much astringent wine as possible. Nor indeed is it fitting to melt it with any oil that comes to hand. Rather, if some redness, or heat generally, prevails in the parts around the ulcer, it should be melted with one of the astringent and cooling oils; such are myrtle oil and quince oil; and if these are not available, the so-called omphacine oil and omotribes oil (oil of unripe olives). If that vein is large and shows by its colour that it contains blood neither red nor good, but rather blacker and more melancholic, one must excise the vein.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κεκοχυμίας

It once happened, when such a 668 vein had been excised, that the ulcer was immediately cicatrized, even though it was a year old, but that in its place, at the incision from which the vein was excised, a malignant ulcer arose, which could not be cicatrized. It is better, therefore, in such cases, having observed the strength of the patient under treatment, to begin with phlebotomy, and next to purge with a drug that evacuates the melancholic humour and, together with it, the phlegmatic as well, since this too sometimes flows in together with the melancholic in the generation of varices. Then, next, to scarify lengthwise the vein itself that lies above the ulcer and, having evacuated in abundance, first to heal this very incision that has been made in the vein; and when it already appears to be perfectly cicatrizing over, then to undertake the treatment of the malignant ulcer, as has been said before. And the spleen too, when it is in a bad condition, sometimes becomes the cause of such cacochymy (κεκοχυμίας, presumably for κακοχυμίας), just as the liver does also; having first made provision for these, one should in this way undertake the cure of the ulcer.

It once happened, when such a vein 668 had been excised, that the ulcer at once cicatrised, although it was a year old, but in its place, at the incision from which the vein had been excised, a malignant ulcer arose which could not be brought to cicatrise. In such cases, then, it is better, after observing the strength of the patient under treatment, to begin with phlebotomy, and next to purge with a drug that evacuates the melancholic humour and, along with it, the phlegmatic as well, since this too sometimes flows in together with the melancholic in the generation of varices. The spleen too, when it is in a bad condition, sometimes becomes the cause of such cacochymy, as indeed does the liver as well; one must first take care of these, and only then undertake the healing of the ulcer.

Greek (Kühn)

κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν λόγον, κᾂν κακόχυμον ἢ πληθωρικὸν ὅλον ᾖ τὸ σῶμα, προκαθῆραί τε καὶ προκενῶσαι πρότερον αὐτὸ, πρὶν ἐπιτιθέναι τῷ ἕλκει 669 φάρμακον. ἐν αὐτῷ δὲ τῷ θεραπεύειν τὸ ἕλκος διὰ τῶν ξηρῶν φαρμάκων ἐφ’ ἑκάστῃ ἐπιλύσει προσέχειν χρὴ τὴν γεγενημένην ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ περὶ τὸ μόριον ἐνέργειαν, ἐπισταμένους τε καὶ μεμνημένους ὅτι τὰ μὲν ἄνευ τοῦ ῥύπτειν ξηραίνοντα, τοιαῦτα δ’ ἐστὶν ἐν οἷς ἡ στύφουσα ποιότης ἐπικρατεῖ, καθαρὸν οὐκ ἐργάζεται τὸ ἕλκος. ὅσα δὲ πρὸς τῷ ξηραίνειν ἔτι καὶ τὸ ῥύπτειν ἔχει, ταῦτα οὐ μόνον ξηρὸν, ἀλλὰ καὶ καθαρὸν ἀποδείκνυσι τὸ ἡλκωμένον ὅλον μόριον. εἰδέναι δὲ χρὴ καὶ ὡς δάκνεται τὰ καθαρὰ τῶν ἑλκῶν, ὑφ’ ὧν ἔμπροσθεν οὐδὲν ἐδάκνετο φαρμάκων, ἡνίκα ἦν ῥυπαρά. μεταβαίνειν οὖν ἀπὸ τῶν ἰσχυρότερον ῥυπτόντων ἐπὶ τὸ πρᾳότερον καὶ ἀδηκτότερον, καθ’ ὃν ἂν καιρὸν ἀκριβῶς καθαρὸν εὑρέθη τὸ ἕλκος, ὥσπερ γε πάλιν ἐπὶ τῶν ῥυπαρῶν, ἐπανέρχεσθαι ἐπὶ τὰ ῥυπτικώτερα, πλὴν εἰ μὴ διὰ τὸ δηχθῆναι ῥυπαρὸν ἐφάνη.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And by the same reasoning, even if the whole body is cacochymous or plethoric, one must first purge it and evacuate it beforehand, before applying to the ulcer 669 a drug. And in the very course of treating the ulcer by means of the dry drugs, at each unbinding one must attend to the action that has been produced by it upon the part, knowing and remembering that those which dry without cleansing — and such are those in which the astringent quality prevails — do not render the ulcer clean; whereas those which, in addition to drying, also possess the power of cleansing, render the whole ulcerated part not only dry but also clean. One must also know that clean ulcers are bitten by drugs by which they were previously not bitten at all, when they were foul. One must therefore pass from the drugs that cleanse more strongly to what is milder and less biting, at whatever moment the ulcer is found to be perfectly clean, just as, conversely, in the case of foul ulcers, one must return to the more cleansing drugs — unless it appeared foul because of having been bitten.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπιλύσει, ἐνέργειαν

By the same reasoning, if the whole body is cacochymous or plethoric, one must first purge and evacuate it beforehand, before applying a drug to the ulcer 669. And in the very course of treating the ulcer by means of the dry drugs, at each removal of the dressing one must attend to the action that the drug has produced on the part. One must know and remember that drugs which dry without deterging do not make the ulcer clean; such are those in which the astringent quality predominates. But those which, besides drying, also have the power to deterge render the whole ulcerated part not only dry but also clean. One must also know that clean ulcers are stung by drugs by which they were not stung at all before, when they were foul. One must therefore pass from the more strongly deterging drugs to the milder and less stinging kind at whatever moment the ulcer is found to be thoroughly clean. Conversely, in foul ulcers one must go back to the more detergent drugs, unless the ulcer has appeared foul because it has been stung.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For this has sometimes deceived even physicians who are reputed to be good, so that they passed over to another drug, more detergent and more acrid, by which the ulcer, being eroded again, 670 appeared at the next undoing of the dressing equally foul or even more so, the flesh that had been melted down by the drug fouling it. For the flesh that is being melted down is dissolved into vicious ichors, and of these, whatever is more fine-particled in its consistency is dispersed by the drug, while the remainder, congealing there around the ulcers, becomes filth for them. One must therefore attend to whether the ulcer became foul because the underlying flesh was being eroded and melted down, or because it needed a drug that removes the filth, while the one applied was not of that kind. The distinction is made by the fact that the ulcer has at the same time become hollower and has its lips reddish and hotter, whenever it is being fouled by the acridity of the drug. Now perhaps it would be superfluous, in the present treatise, to add a description of the manner of using the compound drugs; for it has been stated in the Therapeutic Method. But things that are necessary and that bring great harm if overlooked, it is better to say even twice and three times for the sake of reminding.

Let this, then, also be added to the account: in the treatment of such ulcers, if it should be 671 possible, without harm to the whole body, for the patient to go without bathing, being rubbed down with linen cloths for a longer time, and next with oil, and then exercising with jumping-weights (halteres) if the affected part is a leg, or by running if it is in the parts above. ﻿ 672 ἀπέχεσθαι χρὴ τούτων, ὡς μήτε αὐτὰ καθ’ ἑαυτὰ προσφέρειν μήτε κατὰ τῶν εἰρημένων ξηρῶν φαρμάκων μιγνύναι. κηρὸν οὖν ἐκλέγεσθαι χρὴ μήτε φύσει ξηρὸν μήθ’ ὑπὸ παλαιότητος εἰς τοῦθ’ ἥκοντα, πρόσφατον δὲ καὶ φύσει λιπαρὸν, ὃν ἐν ἡλίῳ μαλάττων τις ἀναλαβεῖν εἰς αὐτὸν δυνήσεται τῶν εἰρημένων ξηρῶν φαρμάκων. μὴ ὄντος δὲ ἡλίου, παρά τε πυρὶ θαλπόμενος μαλάττηται καὶ καθιέμενος εἰς ὕδωρ θερμόν. οὐ γὰρ ἀναμίγνυται δι’ ὅλου τὸ ὕδωρ, ἀλλ’ ἀποῤῥεῖ ταχέως αὐτοῦ μαλαττομένου πρὶν ἐπιτεθῆναι τῷ ἕλκει. συμμετρία δὲ τῆς μίξεώς ἐστι τοιάδε. μέτριον μὲν εἴ τις βούλοιτο γενέσθαι τὸ φάρμακον, τὸ τέταρτον μέρος προσβάλῃ τῷ κηρῷ τῶν μεταλλικῶν, ἰσχυρότερον δὲ ἔσται τοῦ τρίτου μιχθέντος. αὐτῶν δὲ τῶν μεταλλικῶν ἧττόν τε καὶ μᾶλλον ἀδήκτων τε καὶ ξηραντικῶν ὄντων, ἐπιταθήσεται μὲν ἐν τοῖς ξηραντικωτέροις ἡ δύναμις, ἐκλυθήσεται δ’ ἐν τοῖς ἧττον ξηραίνουσιν, ὥσπερ γε πάλιν, ἀσφαλεστέρα μὲν ἡ χρῆσις ἔσται διὰ τῶν ἀδηκτοτέρων σφαλερωτέρα δὲ διὰ τῶν δακνωδεστέρων.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

672 one must abstain from these, so as neither to apply them by themselves nor to mix them into the dry drugs that have been mentioned. One must therefore select a wax that is neither dry by nature nor has come to this condition through age, but fresh and oily by nature, which one will be able, by softening it in the sun, to make take up into itself some of the aforementioned dry drugs. If there is no sun, let it be softened by being warmed at a fire and by being let down into hot water. For the water is not mixed in throughout, but flows off quickly as the wax is softened, before it is applied to the ulcer. The proportion of the mixture is as follows. If one wishes the drug to be moderate, let him add to the wax a fourth part of the metallic substances; it will be stronger if a third is mixed in. And since the metallic substances themselves are to a lesser and a greater degree both non-biting and drying, the power will be intensified in the more drying ones and will be relaxed in those that dry less; just as, again, the use will be safer through the less biting ones and more hazardous through the more biting ones.

672 one must abstain from these, so as neither to apply them by themselves nor to mix them in with the dry drugs already mentioned. When there is no sun, let it be softened by being warmed beside a fire and by being let down into hot water. εἴρηται γὰρ πολλάκις ὡς ἔνιαι μὲν φύσεις σωμάτων αἰσθητικώτερον ἔχουσι τό τε δέρμα καὶ τὰς σάρκας 673 ἐνίαις δὲ δυσαισθητότερα ταῦτ’ ἐστὶν ἄμφω. κατὰ δὲ τὸ μαλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον αἰσθάνεσθαι καὶ τὸ τῆς ὀδύνης πάθος ἀκολουθεῖ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττον, ὥς γε καὶ τὸ τῆς φλεγμονῆς, οὐ μικρὰ δὲ διαφορὰ καὶ παρὰ τὸ μαλακόσαρκον ὑπάρχει ἢ σκληρότερον, εὔχυμόν τε καὶ κακόχυμον, ἤ πληθωρικὸν ἢ κενὸν ἢ μέσως διακείμενον ἐν τοῖς εἰρημένοις. οὐκοῦν οἷόν τε πᾶσιν ἀνθρώποις ἓν ἐπιτήδειον σκευάσαι φάρμακον. ἀλλ’ ὁποῖον ἂν σκευασθῇ τῶν ὁμοειδῶν, ἐπίστασθαι τούτῳ χρῆσθαι προσήκει τεχνωθέντα κα τὰ τὴν ἐν τῷδε τῷ λόγῳ διδασκομένην μέθοδον, οἷον εὐθέως ἐπὶ τῆς προκειμένης ὕλης, ἐὰν ἴσον μὲν ἑκατέρου μίξῃς ἰοῦ τε καὶ χαλκοῦ κεκαυμένου. μέρους δ’ ἑνὸς ἥμισυ στυπτηρίας σχιστῆς, ὡς γενέσθαι τὸ συμπληρούμενον ἐκ τῶν τριῶν ἤτοι δραχ. β΄. καὶ ἡμισείας ἢ οὐγγίας τοσαύτης, ἀρκέσει κηροῦ μῖξαι μὲν δραχ. ι΄. ταῖς δραχμαῖς γο δ’ ι΄. ταῖς οὐγγίαις εἰς μετρίου τε καὶ μέσου τῇ δυνάμει φαρμάκου σύνθεσιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For it has often been said that some natures of bodies have both the skin and the flesh more sensitive, 673 while in some both of these are less sensitive. And in proportion to the greater and lesser degree of sensation, the affection of pain too follows to a greater and lesser degree, as indeed does that of inflammation; and there is no small difference also according to whether the body is soft-fleshed or harder, euchymous or cacochymous, or plethoric or empty or disposed in an intermediate manner among the conditions mentioned. It is not possible, then, to prepare one drug suitable for all human beings. But whichever of the drugs of the same kind has been prepared, it is fitting that one who has been trained according to the Method taught in this treatise should know how to use it; as, for instance, straightaway in the case of the material before us: if you mix an equal amount of each, of verdigris and of burnt copper, and half of one part of split alum, so that the whole made up from the three comes to either two and a half drachms or that many ounces, it will suffice to mix ten drachms of wax with the drachms, and ten ounces with the ounces, for the composition of a drug moderate and intermediate in power.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: γο δ’ ι΄ (read as the ounce sign with the numeral 10; the text appears abbreviated or corrupt), κα τὰ (read as κατὰ), μέρους δ’ ἑνὸς ἥμισυ (taken as 'half of one part')

For it has often been said that some natures of bodies have the skin and the flesh more sensitive, 673 while in others both of these are less sensitive. And according to the greater and lesser degree of sensation, the affection of pain also follows in greater and lesser degree, as does that of inflammation. No small difference also arises from whether the body is soft-fleshed or harder, euchymous or cacochymous, or plethoric or empty or in a middle condition between the states mentioned. It is therefore not possible to prepare one drug suitable for all people. But whatever drug of the same kind is prepared, one who has been trained in the art according to the method taught in this treatise ought to know how to use it. Take, for example, the material now before us: if you mix an equal amount of each, of verdigris and of burnt copper, and half of one part of split alum, so that the total made up from the three is either 2½ drachms or as many ounces, it will suffice to mix in 10 drachms of wax for the drachms, and 10 ounces for the ounces, to compose a drug that is moderate and middling in its power.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διὰ θαλλῶν ἐλαίας

But it will be better and at the same time less biting if it takes diphryges instead of the split alum; and if a whole drachm of this is put in, just as of the 674 burnt copper and the verdigris, the drug will be finer. But if, suspecting the bitingness that comes from the verdigris, we should wish it to become completely non-biting, one must put in the verdigris burnt. And if we wish it also to remove filth, half a drachm of scale of copper must be added, so that the whole proportion becomes as follows. Take burnt copper and burnt verdigris and diphryges, an equal amount of each, that is one drachm apiece, and of scale of copper half a drachm; and let these be taken up in the fatty wax as it is being softened, as has been said before. But if it is drier than is needed for it to receive the things mentioned, one must, while softening it, mix in myrtle oil or quince oil or mastic oil or castor oil or henna oil or old oil of unripe olives (omotribes) or the fresh Spanish oil or the oil prepared among us with olive shoots. But if we should wish not to add anything oily at all to the drug, one must mix in some larch resin. And if we do not have this at hand, terebinth resin must be used in its place.

Greek (Kühn)

ἀεὶ δὲ καὶ τούτου χρὴ μεμνῆσθαι τοὺς ἀναγινώσκοντας ταῦτα, μάλιστα μὲν ἐπὶ τὸ πρῶτον εἰρημένον ὡς ἄριστον 675 ἔρχεσθαι, μετὰ τοῦτο δ’ ἐπὶ τὸ δεύτερόν τε καὶ τρίτον ἐφεξῆς τε τἄλλα τὰ μετὰ τὸ τρίτον, ὡς ἀνάλογον τῆς τῶν φαρμάκων ἀρετῆς τε καὶ δυνάμεως τῇ τάξει τοῦ λόγου χρωμένων ἡμῶν. προακηκοὼς οὖν τις ταῦτα καὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων τὰς δυνάμεις ἐπιστάμενος ἱκανὸς ἔσται διακρίνειν ὅσα τοῖς πρὸ ἡμῶν ἐγράφη πρὸς τὰ κακοήθη τῶν ἑλκῶν φάρμακα, οἷον εὐθέως Ἀσκληπιάδης ὁ φαρμακίων ἐπικληθεὶς ἐν τῷ τρίτῳ τῶν ἐκτὸς οὕτως ἔγραψε. [Περὶ τῶν ὑπ’ Ἀσκληπιάδου γεγραμμένων φαρμάκων.] Πρὸς τὰ χειρώνεια καὶ δυσαλθῆ τῶν ἑλκῶν φάρμακον ἐπιτετευγμένον. ♃ λεπίδος χαλκοῦ οὐγγίαν α΄. ἰοῦ ξυστοῦ οὐγγίαν α΄. κηροῦ ἡμίλιτρον, ῥητίνης λάρικος γο α S". τὰ τηκτὰ κατὰ τῶν ξηρῶν καὶ ἀνελόμενος χρῶ μαλάσσων ἐπιμελῶς καὶ πλατυσμάτιον ἐπιῤῥίπτων κατὰ μόνου τοῦ πεπονθότος, ὥστε περιλαμβάνειν μόνον τὸ ἕλκος. δεῖ δὲ τὰ περὶξ ἀφλεγμάντῳ φαρμάκῳ περιλαμβάνειν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀφλεγμάντῳ

And those who read these things must always bear this too in mind: to resort first and foremost to what was mentioned first as best, 675 after that to the second and the third, and in succession to the others after the third, since we employ the order of the discourse in proportion to the excellence and power of the drugs. Someone, then, who has heard these things beforehand and knows the powers of the simple drugs will be competent to judge all the drugs that were written by those before us against malignant ulcers, as, for instance, straightaway Asclepiades, surnamed Pharmakion, wrote in the third book of his External Remedies as follows. [On the drugs written by Asclepiades.] A well-tested drug for chironian and hard-to-heal ulcers. Take: flake of copper, one ounce; scraped verdigris, one ounce; wax, half a litra; larch resin, one and a half ounces. Pour the melted ingredients over the dry ones, and, having taken it up, use it, softening it carefully and laying a flat plaster (platysmation) upon the affected part alone, so that it covers only the ulcer. The surrounding parts must be covered with a drug that keeps off inflammation.

Those who read these things must always remember this too: to have recourse above all to what was mentioned first, as being best, 675 and after this to the second and the third, and then in order to the others that come after the third, since we have arranged the order of our account in proportion to the excellence and potency of the drugs. Pour the meltable ingredients over the dry ones, and when you have taken it up, use it, softening it carefully and laying a small plaster over the affected part alone, so as to cover only the ulcer. The surrounding parts must be covered with a drug that prevents inflammation.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐν δὲ τῇ θεραπείᾳ λύειν χρὴ διὰ τρίτης τὸ ἕλκος, ἔπειτα προπυριᾷν καὶ 676 τὸ αὐτὸ πλατυσμάτιον ἀπονίπτοντα μαλάσσειν καὶ πάλιν ἐπιτιθέναι, ἔα; ἂν ἄρξηται συνουλώσεως. αὕτη μὲν ἡ τοῦ φαρμάκου γραφὴ, καλῶς προσγράψαντος αὐτοῦ πρὸς τὰ χειρώνεια, καλοῦσι γὰρ οὕτως τὰ μὴ μετρίως κακοήθη τῶν ἑλκῶν, ἀλλὰ τὰ πάνυ σφόδρα τοιαῦτα. γενναῖον δ’ ἐστὶ τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον, ὡς ἂν ἐκ τρίτου μέρους ἔχον τοῦ κηροῦ τὰ μεταλλικὰ, τὸν μὲν ἰὸν ἰσχυρότερον φάρμακον, τὴν δὲ λεπίδα τοῦ χαλκοῦ, μετριώτερον μὲν πολὺ τοῦ ἰοῦ, γενναῖον δὲ ὅμως καὶ αὐτό. συμμέτρως δ’ εἴπερ εἶχε κράσεως ὡς καὶ τὰ μετρίως κακοήθη θεραπεύειν, οὐ τριπλάσιος, ἀλλὰ τετραπλάσιος ὁ κηρὸς ἐμέμικτο τοῖς ἄλλοις, ἔτι δ’ ἂν μετριώτερον, εἰ πενταπλάσιος, ὡς καὶ τὰ μηδέπω κακοήθη, δυσεπούλωτα δὲ ἄλλως ὄντα θεραπεύειν. ὥσπερ γὰρ κᾂν εἰ τὸν κηρὸν ἑξαπλάσιον ἐργάσαιτό τις τῶν μεταλλικῶν, ὡς εἶναι τοῦ μὲν ἰοῦ καὶ τῆς λεπίδος ἑκατέρου δραχμὴν, κηροῦ δὲ δραχ. ιβ΄. τῆς λάρικος δὲ τὸ ἀνάλογον. ἀνάλογον δὲ ἔσται, εἰ τρεῖς εἶεν αὐτῆς δραχμαί.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἔα;

In the treatment one should unbind the ulcer every third day, then foment it beforehand and, 676 washing off the same flat plaster, soften it and apply it again, until (ἔα; ἂν, apparently for ἕως ἂν) it begins cicatrization. This is the written prescription of the drug, and he rightly added to it "for Chironian ulcers"; for they call thus those ulcers that are not moderately malignant, but very extremely so. This drug is strong, inasmuch as it contains the metallic ingredients at a third part of the wax—the verdigris being the stronger drug, and the scale of copper much more moderate than the verdigris, yet strong too in itself. But if it were to have a proportioned mixture so as to treat also the moderately malignant ulcers, the wax would have been mixed with the other ingredients not threefold but fourfold; and it would be still more moderate if fivefold, so as to treat also those ulcers that are not yet malignant but are otherwise difficult to cicatrize. Just as, too, if one were to make the wax sixfold the metallic ingredients, so that there is one drachm of each, the verdigris and the scale, and twelve drachms of wax, and the proportionate amount of the larch resin. And it will be proportionate if there are three drachms of it.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἔα;, ὥσπερ γὰρ κᾂν, διὰ τρίτης

In the treatment one must undo the ulcer every third day, then foment it beforehand and, washing off 676 the same small plaster, soften it and apply it again, until (ἔα; = presumably ἕως) cicatrisation begins. This, then, is the prescription of the drug, and he rightly added in the heading 'for Chironian ulcers'; for that is what they call ulcers that are not moderately malignant but extremely so. This drug is powerful, since it has the metallic ingredients in a third part of the wax: verdigris, a rather strong drug, and flake of copper, much more moderate than the verdigris but nonetheless powerful itself as well. If it had a well-proportioned mixture, so as to treat also moderately malignant ulcers, the wax would have been mixed with the other ingredients not threefold but fourfold; and it would be more moderate still if fivefold, so as to treat also ulcers that are not yet malignant but are otherwise hard to cicatrise. For so too (ὥσπερ γὰρ κἂν) if one were to make the wax sixfold the metallic ingredients, so that there is one drachm each of the verdigris and the flake, and of wax 12 drachms, and of the larch resin the proportional amount. And it will be proportional if there are three drachms of it.

λιπαροῦ δ’ ὄντος τοῦ κηροῦ, κᾂν ἔλαττον ἐμβληθῇ τῆς ῥητίνης, αὐτάρκη σύστασιν ﻿ 677 ἕξει τὸ φάρμακον. ἐπὶ γάρ τοι τῶν τοιούτων φαρμάκων ἡ ῥητίνη διὰ τὴν σύστασιν ἐμβάλλεται, καθάπερ γε καὶ τὸ μύρσινον ἢ σχίνινον. ἐγὼ δὲ καὶ Ἀττικῷ ποτε οἰσύπῳ μαλάττων τὸν κηρὸν ἔμιξα. καί μοι κατὰ τὴν χρῆσιν ἐφάνη τὸ φάρμακον ἢ οὐδὲν ἢ βραχὺ παντελῶς ἀπολείπεσθαι τῶν ἄνευ τούτων σκευασθέντων. εὔδηλον δὲ ὡς εὐτονίαν λέγω τοῦ γενησομένου φαρμάκου ταῦτα. τὸ γὰρ τῆς ὠφελείας ἐν τῷ πρός τι, δυναμένου τοῦ μαλακωτέρου φαρμάκου πρὸς τὰ μετριώτερα τῶν κακοήθων ἁρμόττειν, μᾶλλον τοῦ σφοδροτέρου. ἔνθα γοῦν ἧττον μέν ἐστι κακόηθες αὐτὸ τὸ ἕλκος, ὀχθώδη δὲ τὰ χείλη, βελτίων ἐπὶ τούτων ἐστὶν ὁ οἰσύπος. ἀγαθὸν δ’ εἰς ταῦτα φάρμακόν ἐστι καὶ ὁ τῆς ἐλάφου τε καὶ μόσχου μυελὸς, ὅντινα χρὴ προλειώσαντα μιγνύναι τῷ κηρῷ πρότερον, εἶθ’ οὕτως αὐτοῖς ἡνωμένοις ἀναλαμβάνειν τὰ μεταλλικὰ προλελειωμένα, μέχρι τοῦ χνοώδη γενέσθαι. δακνώδη γάρ ἐστι τὰ τοιαῦτα. καὶ πάντα δ’ ἱκανῶς προλελειώσθω τὰ ξηρὰ καὶ γεώδη τῇ συστάσει φάρμακα, καθάπερ ἥ τε κηκίς ἐστι καὶ οἱ κύτινοι καὶ τὰ σίδια καὶ ὁ τῆς Αἰγυπτίας ἀκάνθης καρπός.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὀχθώδη, χνοώδη

But since the wax is fatty, even if less of the resin is put in, the drug will have sufficient consistency 677. For in drugs of this kind the resin is put in for the sake of the consistency, just as indeed the myrtle oil or the mastic oil is too. I myself once also mixed in Attic oesypum while softening the wax, and in use the drug appeared to me to fall short either not at all or only very slightly of those prepared without these. It is quite evident that I say these things with regard to the vigour of the drug that will result; for the matter of benefit lies in relation to something, since the softer drug is able to suit the more moderate of the malignant ulcers more than the more vehement one. At any rate, where the ulcer itself is less malignant but its lips are callous and raised, the oesypum is better in these cases. A good drug for these is also the marrow of the deer and of the calf, which one must first triturate and mix with the wax beforehand, and then, once they have been united in this way, take up with them the metallic substances, previously triturated until they become fine as dust; for such things are biting. And let all the drugs that are dry and earthy in consistency be sufficiently triturated beforehand, such as oak-gall, the pomegranate flowers (kytinoi), the pomegranate rinds, and the fruit of the Egyptian thorn (akantha).

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πλατυσμάτιον, ἀποπυρία

For indeed all such things are hard to triturate, 678 just as those made from dried juices or saps are easy to triturate — myrrh and aloe and acacia and all that are of the same genus as these. And Asclepiades rightly added, at the end of the written recipe of the aforesaid drug, in these very words: 'and undo these every third day and foment, and, having washed off the same small spread plaster, soften it and apply it again.' For unless the drug remains in contact with the skin for a longer time, it will accomplish nothing. This very point too has escaped most physicians, and they think that, if they wipe away the ichors of the ulcer three times a day, they are doing something better than those who wipe them away twice. And those who undo the dressing once a day the patients themselves often blame, as though they were being neglected. But it is fitting that you remember here too what has often been said by us in many of our treatises, that the qualities of all bodies that come into proximity act in some way upon one another, even if one of them is much the stronger. For indeed according to this principle they become in a way of the same kind with one another in the course of time, even if they differ very greatly.

For all such things are hard to pulverise 678, just as those that come from dried juices or saps are easy to pulverise: myrrh, aloe, acacia and whatever is of the same genus as these. For unless the drug stays in contact with the skin for a longer time, it will accomplish nothing. This very point too has escaped most physicians, and they think that if they wipe away the ichors of the ulcer three times a day, they are doing something better than those who wipe them off twice. And those who unbind the dressing only once are often blamed by the patients themselves for neglecting them. But here too you ought to remember what we have often said in many of our treatises, that the qualities of all bodies that lie near one another act in some degree upon one another, even if one of them is much the stronger. For by this reasoning they become in time somehow of the same kind as one another, even if they differ very greatly.

And when the quality of the drug has become of like kind with the body being treated, the benefit 679 that follows is greater. For these reasons, then, the one who first thought of using the flat piece (platysma) that had been applied before would justly be praised, and it was fitting for us to follow him all the more, since we have also recognised that this idea is attested by experience. And reasonably he also orders that it be fomented every third day—that is, evidently, at each unbinding—for since the drug is strong, it naturally requires soothing. It is better, therefore, in cases that are not very malignant, that the wax be fourfold or fivefold of both the metallic substances taken together. Next after the drug written above Asclepiades has written another drug having the following composition: Take verdigris, 2 ounces; scale, 2 ounces; diphryges, 1 ounce; wax, 1 pound; larch resin, 3 ounces. he thought it right to put in of it; the resin, however, 680 at half an ounce beyond each of the metallic ingredients. In the second recipe, of this (the resin) he orders three ounces to be put in instead of one and a half, and of the metallic ingredients two instead of one ounce of each, and a litra of wax, whereas in the former recipe he put in half a litra. It is quite evident, then, that in the second he added only the diphryges, and since he orders an ounce of it to be put in in proportion to the doubled quantities of the others, if someone, mixing in single amounts all the ingredients of the former recipe, adds half an ounce of diphryges to them, he will make the composition of the drug as follows: take scale, 1 ounce; verdigris, 1 ounce; wax, half a litra; larch resin, one ounce and a half; diphryges, half an ounce. This too comes out the same as the former in every respect, except for the diphryges. Diphryges is a drug so moderate that even sprinkled on by itself it heals those ulcers that are hard to cicatrize; and burnt copper is similar to it. But verdigris is considerably stronger, so that it has fallen outside the range of treating even the very foul and moist ulcers, if one should sprinkle it on by itself; for it erodes and bites violently.

For since this has been omitted, it comes about that most physicians use them 682 badly, and for this reason at times hit the mark wonderfully, while at other times they also fail, falling very far short. And after it again he writes six 683 others, prefixing to each the word "another," evidently bidding us to understand in addition the word "cicatrizing." Now five of them belong to the customary cicatrizing drugs, neither for ulcers that are hard to cicatrize nor for malignant ones, while the sixth belongs to those for ulcers hard to cicatrize, though not yet indeed to those for malignant ones. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πρῶιον, ὡς ἂν ἴσον ἔχον ὄγκον, διφθέραν ἑαυτοῦ σκληρὰν

Of these things he did none; instead he wrote, one after another, 684 six cicatrizing preparations, whose composition I shall state together with the appropriate distinctions. Now the first one written by him, the seventh from the beginning, is quite strong, inasmuch as it received an equal bulk of verdigris and of scale; but the admixture of wax has relaxed and diminished its strength, being eightfold of either and fourfold of both together. For he bids one mix an ounce of each of the verdigris and the scale, and eight ounces of wax, the composer of it having very properly guarded against mixing in any of the emollient things—I mean oil and fat, oesypum, butter and resin. But if the wax is not oily, it will hold the metallic substances hard to mix, and even if we force their mixture, doing it either beside a fire or in hot sun, a little later the drug will be hard, so that when it is applied to the ulcer the patient will think he is touching a hard hide of his own.

He did none of these things, but wrote, one after another, 684 six cicatrizing remedies, whose composition I shall state together with the appropriate distinctions. The first one written by him, then (the seventh from the beginning), is quite strong, inasmuch as it has taken verdigris and scale in equal bulk; but the admixture of the wax has slackened and reduced its strength, being eightfold of each and fourfold of both together. For he bids mix an ounce of each of the verdigris and the scale, and eight ounces of the wax, the one who compounded it having very rightly taken care not to mix in any of the emollient substances, I mean oil and fat, oesypum and butter and resin. But if the wax is not oily, it will have the metallic ingredients hard to mix in, and even if we force their mixing, doing it either by the fire or in hot sun, a little later the drug will become hard, so that when it is applied to the ulcer the patient will think a hard hide is touching him.

Greek (Kühn)

πάντα γὰρ τὰ τοιαῦτα φάρμακα τὴν χρῆσιν ἔχει τοιαύτην, ἢν καὶ θερμὰς ἔχων τις τὰς χεῖρας ἀναιρεῖται τηλικοῦτον μέγεθος ἐξ αὐτῶν, ἡλίκον μαλαχθέν τε καὶ πλατυνθὲν, ἐπιτεθήσεται καθ’ ὅλου τοῦ ἕλκους, 685 ὡς καὶ τὰ χείλη σκέπειν. ὅταν δ’ ᾖ βαθὺ, προσαναπληροῦται τὸ κοῖλον αὐτοῦ πᾶν, ἐντεθέντος αὐτῷ τοῦ φαρμάκου. καὶ μετὰ τοῦτο πάλιν ἐπιτίθεται πλάτυσμα ἕτερον, συμπεριλαμβάνον καὶ τὰ χείλη. κᾄπειτα τούτῳ βεβρεγμένον πάλιν ὀθόνιον ὕδατι ψυχρῷ τηλικοῦτον ἐπιβάλλεται κατὰ παντὸς, ὡς σκεπάσαι τὸ φάρμακον, εἶτ’ ἔξωθεν σπόγγος μαλακὸς, ἐπιδέσμῳ κατειλημμένος ἐξ ὀθόνης, ἤτοι ἅπαξ ἢ δὶς ἐν κύκλῳ περὶ τὸ πεπονθὸς μόριον ἑλιττομένῳ, τηλικούτῳ τὸ πλάτος, ὡς ἅπαν κατειλῆφθαι τὸ ἕλκος ἅμα τοῖς χείλεσι, καί τι καὶ τῶν ὑπερκειμένων τε καὶ ὑποκειμένων αὐτοῖς, ἡ δὲ ἐπίδεσις ἀπὸ δυοῖν ἀρχῶν γιγνέσθω, καλοῦσι δ’ οὕτως, ὅταν τὸ μέσον ὅλον τοῦ τελαμῶνος ἐπιβάλληται τῷ πεπονθότι τόπῳ, τῶν κεφαλῶν δ’ ἑκάτερον ἀπ άγηται πρὸς τὸ ἀντικείμενον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἢν

For all such drugs have the following manner of use: in which (ἢν) one, having also warmed one's hands, takes up from them a mass of such a size as, when softened and flattened out, will be laid over the whole of the ulcer, 685 so as to cover the lips as well. And when it is deep, the whole of its hollow is filled up in addition, the drug being inserted into it. And after this another flat piece (platysma) is laid on in turn, encompassing the lips too. And then upon this in turn a linen cloth soaked in cold water is laid over the whole, of such a size as to cover the drug; then, on the outside, a soft sponge, secured by a bandage of linen cloth wound once or twice in a circle around the affected part, of such a width that the whole ulcer is encompassed together with the lips, and also something of the parts lying above and below them; and let the bandaging be done 'from two heads'—they call it so when the whole middle of the band is laid upon the affected place, and each of the heads is drawn away toward the opposite side.

For all such drugs have a use of this kind: one who has warm hands takes up from them a portion of a size such as, once softened and spread flat, will be laid over the whole ulcer, 685 so as to cover the lips as well. When the ulcer is deep, its whole hollow is filled up besides, the drug being put into it. After this another flat spread is laid on, taking in the lips as well. Then over this a piece of linen soaked in cold water, again of such a size, is laid over the whole, so as to cover the drug. Then, on the outside, a soft sponge, held in place by a bandage of linen cloth wound in a circle around the affected part either once or twice, and of such a breadth that the whole ulcer is taken in together with its lips, and also something of the parts lying above and below them. Let the bandaging be done 'from two starting-points': they call it this when the whole middle of the band is laid upon the affected place, and each of the heads is led away towards the opposite side.

Greek (Kühn)

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑπὲρ τῶν μὴ μαθόντων

Then, if the linen bandage is strong enough to hold the sponge securely with a single turn, it will be sewn together there at its ends, the excess being cut away with scissors; but if it is weak, each of the heads will be brought once more over the affected part, drawn with equal tension—which is among the things most to be observed both in the first turn 686 and no less also in the second. Nor indeed should you forget that which Hippocrates, the first of those we know, discovered, a point in which error was formerly made by all before him and is now made by (ὑπὲρ) those who have not learned the art in the proper manner. For they think it better to keep the ulcerated part free from pressure and to wrap the bandage around on either side of it—and how harmful this is was shown by Hippocrates. So when these things have been done by you on the first day, on the second soak the sponge thoroughly, pouring cold water over it. If the surrounding air is hot and dry, this must be done even twice and three times a day, and the bandage must be undone every third day; but if it is winter, every fourth day. One must examine the alteration that has come about from the drug around the affected place, both in the cavity of the ulcer and at the lips, and indeed round about them. And let the examination be made by you on the basis of what you learned in the treatises On Temperaments; these ulcers have also been discussed in the Method of Healing.

For it has appeared either drier or moister or hotter 687 or colder than before, both the affected part as a whole and each of its parts. For all these are acrid when unburnt, but when burnt 688 they are moderate, and when washed after the burning they become non-biting. Greek (Kühn)

ἡ λεπὶς δὲ ἰσχυρότερον καὶ τραχυντικώτερον καὶ δηκτικώτερον φάρμακόν ἐστι, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο λιθάργυρον ἔμιξεν ὁ συνθεὶς τὸ φάρμακον, ὑπειδόμενος 689 τὸ βίαιον τῆς λεπίδος, ἐκλῦσαί τε βουληθεὶς αὐτὸ τῇ μίξει τοῦ μαλακωτέρου φαρμάκου. καὶ εἴ τις ἄνευ κηροῦ τὰ τέτταρα μίξειε κατὰ τὴν εἰρημένην συμμετρίαν, ἕξει φάρμακον ξηρὸν ἐπίπαστον οὐκ ἀγεννὲς εἰς τὴν τῶν κακοήθων ἑλκῶν ἴασιν. ἀλλ’ ἔμπλαστρόν γε βουλομένῳ σοι ποιῆσαι τόν τε κηρὸν ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστι μιγνύναι. καὶ διὰ τὸ τῶν μεταλλικῶν πλῆθος ἐξ ἀνάγκης καὶ τῶν ὑγρῶν ἐμνημόνευσεν ὁ Ἀνδρόμαχος ἐν τῇ προκειμένῃ δυνάμει ῥοδίνου τε καὶ μυρσίνου. πάνυ γὰρ ὁ κηρὸς ὀλίγος ἐστὶν, ὡς πρὸς τὸ τῶν μεταλλικῶν πλῆθος. ὅπου τοίνυν τῆς χρυσοκόλλης μὲν καὶ τῆς λεπίδος καὶ τοῦ διφρυγοῦς, ἐκ δυοῖν δραχμῶν τῶν εἰρημένων, τῆς λιθαργύρου δὲ ὀκτὼ μίγνυσθαι κελεύει, τεσσαρεσκαίδεκα δηλονότι τὰς πάσας ἐργαζόμενος ἢ κηρὸν λιπαρὸν ἐδεῖτο μῖξαι πάμπολυ ἢ προσμῖξαί τι τῷ φαρμάκῳ τῶν ἐλαιωδῶν. ἐπὶ δὲ τοῦ προειρημένου φαρμάκου τετραπλάσιος ἦν ὁ κηρὸς τῶν μεταλλικῶν. ἔξεστιν οὖν ἡμῖν ἀφελοῦσι τὴν λιθάργυρον ἐκ τῶν ἄλλων συντιθέναι τὸ φάρμακον, ὥσπερ ἐγώ ποτε ἐποίησα. εἰ γὰρ τοῦτο πράξειέ τις, οὐ δεήσεται τοῦ μυρσίνου, λιπαροῦ γε, ὡς ἔφην, ὄντος τοῦ κηροῦ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But the scale is a stronger, more roughening and more biting drug, and for this reason the man who composed the drug mixed in litharge, viewing with suspicion 689 the violent character of the scale and wishing to relax it by the admixture of the softer drug. And if someone were to mix the four without wax according to the stated proportion, he will have a dry drug for sprinkling on, not ignoble for the cure of malignant ulcers. But if you wish to make a plaster, it is necessary to mix in the wax as well. And on account of the quantity of the metallic substances Andromachus of necessity also made mention of the liquids in the formula before us, both rose oil and myrtle oil. For the wax is very small in amount relative to the quantity of the metallic substances. Where, then, he orders that chrysocolla, scale and diphryges be mixed in, two drachmas each of those mentioned, and eight of litharge, making fourteen in all, evidently, he needed either to mix in a very great deal of fatty wax or to add to the drug something of the oily substances. In the previously mentioned drug, however, the wax was four times the metallic substances. It is therefore possible for us, having removed the litharge, to compound the drug from the others, as I myself once did. For if someone does this, he will not need the myrtle oil, provided, as I said, that the wax is fatty.

The scale, however, is a stronger, more roughening and more biting drug, and for this reason the composer of the drug mixed in litharge, being wary of 689 the violence of the scale and wishing to relax it by the admixture of the softer drug. And if one should mix the four without wax in the stated proportion, he will have a dry dusting-powder drug that is not contemptible for the cure of malignant ulcers. But if you wish to make a plaster, it is necessary to mix in the wax as well. And because of the quantity of the metallic substances, Andromachus of necessity also mentioned liquids in the preparation under discussion, namely rose oil and myrtle oil. For the wax is very little in relation to the quantity of the metallic substances. Where, then, he directs that chrysocolla, scale and diphryges be mixed at two drachmas each of those named, and litharge at eight, plainly making fourteen in all, he needed either to mix in a very large amount of greasy wax or to add to the drug some of the oily substances. In the previously mentioned drug, by contrast, the wax was four times the metallic substances. It is therefore open to us to remove the litharge and compose the drug from the others, as I myself once did. For if one does this, he will not need the myrtle oil, since the wax, as I said, is greasy.

But if it should be 690 hard, it will be necessary to add something of the liquids as well. For this reason, then, some seem to me to have added such specifications to the wax in drugs of this sort too, some writing "new" wax and others "fresh," not, by Zeus, because they require its age, as in the case of other drugs, but its moistness. For new wax is indeed really better, but not to such a degree that one should already avoid that of two or three years. It is now time to go on to the plaster written next after this one, which runs thus. Rx: diphryges, 84 drachms; litharge, 240 drachms; wax, 318 drachms; oil, 6 kotylai. This drug would be cicatrizing, for ulcers that are not yet malignant but already hard to cicatrize. For it dries, but not strongly, as does the one written after it; nor indeed is it fitting to prepare it in so large a proportion. For this is the same as it: Rx: diphryges, 14 drachms; litharge, 40 drachms; wax, 53 drachms; myrtle oil, 10 ounces. For the quantities written by Andromachus are six times these. This one, then, is truly cicatrizing, similar to the one made with cadmia and to the one called "sweet," about which an account will be given in the following book.

But the one written after 691 it is stronger, yet not close to the first of these six, which is seventh from the very beginning, into which he mixed both scale of copper and verdigris with a fourfold amount of wax. And he says that Zeno mixes in half a drachm of galbanum, or, according to another reading, 4.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὀχθώδη, μιγνύντα

If [he mixed it in] with a view to softening the callous and hard lips, which for this reason are called 692 by some 'bank-like' (ὀχθώδη), he mixes it in rightly; but if with a view to the malignancy of the ulcer, not correctly. And even if it does not take galbanum in addition, even so it falls short of the strength [required] for malignant ulcers. It is therefore more plausible that he wrote these twelve drugs one after another as cicatrizing ones. For with the exception of two among them, the one that is seventh in order from the beginning and the one after it, all the rest are curative of ulcers that are difficult to cicatrize, but no longer of malignant ones. For indeed the twelfth after them is likewise cicatrizing, but too weak to treat malignant ulcers. The composition of this one too is of the following sort. ℞ molybdaena 100 dr., diphryges 16 dr., sory 10 dr., roasted Cyprian misy 8 dr., terebinth resin 8 dr., wax 40 dr., myrtle oil 40 dr. One should compound all drugs of this kind in a smaller proportion having the same ratio as this one, since you know that they quickly become hard and stony. It is therefore better to prepare only so much of each as there is need of.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀχθώδη, ὣς γε, διὰ βραχυτέρας δὲ συμμετρίας

If he mixes it in with a view to softening the callous and hard lips [of the ulcer], which for this reason are called 'ochthōdē' (mound-like) 692 by some, he does well to mix it; but if with a view to the malignancy of the ulcer, he does not do so rightly. And even if it does not take the galbanum in addition, even so it still falls short of the strength required for malignant ulcers. It was therefore more reasonable that he wrote these twelve drugs one after another as cicatrizing. For apart from two among them, the seventh in order from the beginning and the one after it, all the others are curative of ulcers that are difficult to cicatrize, but not of malignant ones. For the twelfth, following these, is likewise cicatrizing, but too weak to treat malignant ones. Its composition is as follows. ℞ Molybdaena 100 dr., diphryges 16 dr., sory 10 dr., roasted Cyprian misy 8 dr., terebinth resin 8 dr., wax 40 dr., myrtle oil 40 dr. All such drugs should be compounded in a smaller quantity, in a proportion analogous to this one, since you know that they quickly become hard and stony. It is better, therefore, to prepare of each only as much as there is need for.
