καὶ μάλιστα ἐὰν χειμὼν διαδέχηται τὴν χρῆσιν 693 αὐτῶν. εἰ δὲ τέταρτον μέρος ἑκάστου τῶν εἰρημένων λαμβάνοις, τῆς μὲν μολυβδαίνης πέντε καὶ εἴκοσι ἔσονται δραχμαὶ, τέσσαρες δὲ τοῦ διφρυγοῦς, δύο δὲ καὶ ἡμίσεια τοῦ σώρεως, τοῦ δὲ ὀπτοῦ μίσυος δύο καὶ τῆς τερμινθίνης δραχ. θ΄. ἄλλως β΄. κηροῦ δὲ καὶ μυρσίνου τὸ ἴσον ἑκατέρου δραχ. ι΄. συναμφότεροι δὲ δηλονότι τῶν εἴκοσιν. ἐπὶ πάντων δὲ τῶν τοιούτων φαρμάκων τήξας τὰ τηκτὰ καὶ ψύξας, ἔπειτα ξύσας μίγνυε τοῖς ξηροῖς ἀκριβῶς λελειωμένοις. ἐὰν γὰρ ἐπικαταχέῃς αὐτοῖς τὰ τηκτὰ, συμβαίνει πολλάκις ἐγκηρίδας γίγνεσθαι, καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν ᾖ κρύος. εἰ δ’ ἐν ἡλίῳ θερμῷ τὴν μίξιν ποιοῖς, καὶ καταχεῖν ἐγχωρεῖ κινοῦντα σφοδρῶς, τὰ μὲν πρῶτα τῇ σπάθῃ, τῷ δοίδυκι δ’ ὀλίγον ὕστερον. ἄμεινον δὲ ἐπὶ τῶν τοιούτων πάντων τὴν σπάθην φοίνικος εἶναι κλάδον. ταῦτα μὲν οὖν ὁ Ἀνδρόμαχος ἔγραψεν, ὡς ἔφην, ἀδιορίστως ιβ΄. φάρμακα προγράψας αὐτῶν κοινῇ τοῦτο μόνον, ὥς ἐστιν ἐπουλωτικά. τὴν διαφορὰν δ’ αὐτῶν ἐξηγησάμην ἐγὼ σαφῶς ὡς νομίζω, ποδηγῆσαι δυναμένην ἄνδρα συνετὸν εἰς τὴν κατὰ μέρος ἐπιδέξιον χρῆσιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δραχ. θ΄. ἄλλως β΄.

and especially if winter follows upon the time of their use 693. If you take a fourth part of each of the ingredients mentioned, there will be twenty-five drachms of molybdaena, four of diphryges, two and a half of sory, two of roasted misy, and of terebinth 9 drachms (otherwise 2), and of wax and myrtle oil an equal amount of each, 10 drachms, both together obviously making up the twenty. In the case of all such drugs, melt the meltable ingredients and cool them, then scrape them and mix them with the dry ingredients, which have been thoroughly triturated. For if you pour the melted ingredients over them, it often happens that enkerides (lumps of wax) are formed, and especially when it is cold. But if you make the mixture in a hot sun, it is also possible to pour them over while stirring vigorously, at first with the spathe, and a little later with the pestle. In all such cases it is better that the spathe be a branch of palm. These, then, are the twelve drugs which Andromachus wrote down, as I said, without distinction, having prefixed to them in common only this, that they are cicatrizing. But I have explained the difference between them clearly, as I think, in a way capable of guiding an intelligent man to their skilful use in particular cases.

Apart from 694 these, he also mentioned other polychrēsta (drugs of many uses), which, just as they are suitable for many other conditions, are so also for that of ulcers hard to cicatrize; these I too shall mention in the following book, where I go through the polychrēsta. I shall now set down the whole of it according to the wording of Andromachus. 695 Cicatrizing remedy of Dioscorides for the chironian ulcers. Take chrysocolla, 2 drachms; scraped verdigris, 2 drachms; scale, 2 drachms; wax, 6 drachms; diphryges, 2 drachms; litharge, 8 drachms; a little rose-oil or myrtle-oil, enough for melting. This drug is exactly the same as the one written earlier, which, among the twelve cicatrizing drugs, comes in order after the one made from scale and verdigris, the eighth among them, as I said; it surpasses that one in a single thing only, the verdigris, and for this reason he wrote that one among the cicatrizing drugs, whereas for this one he made a greater promise, saying that it is suitable for the chironian ulcers. And indeed at this point too he mentions a most excellent drug, writing as follows. Cicatrizing remedy from the writings of Primion, for desperate cases. He intends the term 'desperate' to signify those ulcers which many, having striven to treat, did not attain their end, but abandoned as incurable. One should have confidence in this drug, which, among other things, has been tested by experience. He writes its composition thus: Take sory, 24 drachms.

στυπτηρίας σχιστῆς 696 δραχ. ιστ΄. σιδίων δραχ. ιστ΄. ἀσβέστου δραχ. ιστ΄. λιβάνου δραχ. λβ΄. κηκίδος δραχ. λβ΄. κηροῦ δραχ. ρκ΄. στέατος μοσχείου δραχ. ρξ΄. ἐλαίου παλαιοῦ κοτύλην α΄. αὕτη μὲν ἡ συμμετρία τῶν μιγνυμένων ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων, εἰ τὴν τοῦ προκειμένου συνθέτου κατασκευήν. ὅπως δὲ χρὴ σκευάζειν αὐτὸ παρέλιπεν, ὥσπερ καὶ ἐπὶ ἄλλων πολλῶν ἀναγκαίαν ἐχόντων τὴν διήγησιν τῆς σκευασίας. ἐπὶ τούτου μὲν γὰρ καὶ πάνυ ῥᾴδιός ἐστιν, ὡς ὀλίγον ὕστερον εἰρήσεται. γινώσκειν δὲ ὑμᾶς βούλομαι πρὸ πάντων ὡς ἐὰν μὴ παρείη ποτὲ μόσχειον στέαρ, ἀντ’ αὐτοῦ μιγνύναι τὸ παλαιὸν ὕειον, ὃ καλοῦσιν ἀξούγγιον. ἄναλον δ’ εἶναι χρὴ τοῦτο καὶ μὴ πάνυ πολλῶν ἐτῶν. ἔστιν οὖν καὶ τὸ παλαιὸν ἔλαιον ἱκανῶς διαφορητικὸν, ἔστι δὲ καὶ τὸ στέαρ ἀδήκτως ἄμφω τοῦτο δρῶντα. τὸ δὲ βόειον ὁμοίως μέν ἐστι διαφορητικὸν, ἄδηκτον δ’ οὐχ ὁμοίως. ἐν τῷ μεταξὺ δ’ ἀμφοῖν ἐστι τὸ μόσχειον, ὅσῳ τοῦ χοιρείου δριμύτερόν ἐστι, τοσούτῳ τοῦ βοείου μαλακώτερον ὑπάρχει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: εἰ τὴν τοῦ προκειμένου συνθέτου κατασκευήν

of split alum 696 16 drachms, of pomegranate rinds 16 drachms, of quicklime 16 drachms, of frankincense 32 drachms, of oak-gall 32 drachms, of wax 120 drachms, of calf's fat 160 drachms, of old oil 1 kotyle. This, then, is the proportion of the simple drugs that are mixed together for the preparation of the compound in question. But how one ought to prepare it he omitted, just as also in the case of many others that require an account of their preparation. For in the case of this one it is in fact quite easy, as will be stated a little later. But I want you to know before all else that if calf's fat should at some time not be at hand, one should mix in its place old pig's fat, which they call axoungion. This must be unsalted and not of very many years. Now old oil too is sufficiently discutient, and so too is the fat, both of them doing this without biting. Ox fat is similarly discutient, but not similarly non-biting. Calf's fat lies between both of them: by as much as it is more acrid than pig's fat, by so much is it milder than ox fat.

Greek (Kühn)

τὸ μὲν οὖν φάρμακον, ὑπὲρ οὗ πρόκειται λέγειν, ἰσχυρότατα μὲν ἔχει σῶρυ καὶ τὴν σχιστὴν στυπτηρίαν καὶ ﻿ 697 τὰ σίδια καὶ τὴν κηκίδα, οὐ μὴν διαφορητικήν γε δύναμιν ἔχοντα γενναίαν, ἀλλὰ τὴν καλουμένην στυπτικήν. εὔδηλον, δ’ ὅτι κηκίδα μὲν ἀεὶ βάλλειν προσήκει τὴν ὀμφακῖτιν ὀνομαζομένην, στυπτηρίαν δὲ τὴν Αἰγυπτίαν τε καὶ σχιστήν. εἰ δὲ ἀπορήσαιμεν αὐτῆς, τὴν στρογγύλην παχυμερεστέραν μὲν οὖσαν τῆς σχιστῆς, οὐχ ἧττον δὲ στύφουσαν. ὅτι δὲ σίδια τὰ τῆς ῥοιᾶς λέμματα καλεῖται πάντες γινώσκουσιν. ὀλιγοχρόνιός γε μήν ἐστι τῆς ἀκμῆς αὐτῶν ὁ καιρὸς, ἐάν τις θέλῃ ξηρὰ κόπτειν τε καὶ λειοῦν. ἅμα γὰρ τῷ ξηραίνεσθαι καὶ τὴν δύναμιν ἀσθενεστέραν ἔχει τὰ σίδια μετὰ τῆς διαφορουμένης ὑγρότητος αὐτῶν ἀπολλυμένην. ἀσφαλέστερον οὖν ἐστιν ἔγχυλα λαβόντα προαφεψεῖν ἐν οἴνῳ μέχρις ἂν εὐλείωτα γένηται. χρὴ δὲ τὸν οἶνον αὐστηρὸν μὲν εἶναι πάντως, ἀλλὰ μήτε λίαν νέον· οἱ γὰρ αὐστηροὶ παχυμερεῖς εἰσι, πρὶν χρονισθῆναι· μήτε λίαν παλαιὸν, ὡς ἤδη δριμὺν ὑπάρχειν αὐτόν. ὁ μὲν γὰρ νέος οὐκ εἰσδύεται τῷ βάθει τοῦ σώματος, ὁ δὲ παλαιὸς δριμὺς καὶ δακνώδης ἐστίν. ἀμείνων οὖν ὁ μέσος κατὰ τὴν ἡλικίαν. εἴη δ’ ἄν οὗτος τοὐπίπαν ἐν τῷ μεταξὺ τοῦ τε πενταετοῦς καὶ τοῦ δεκαετοῦς.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

The drug, then, about which it is proposed to speak, has as its strongest ingredients sory and split alum and 697 pomegranate rinds and oak-gall, which however do not have a vigorous dispersive power, but the so-called astringent one. It is quite clear that one should always put in the oak-gall called omphakitis, and the alum that is Egyptian and split. If we should be without it, then the round kind, which is coarser in its parts than the split but no less astringent. That the rinds of the pomegranate are called sidia everyone knows. Yet the season of their prime is short-lived, if one wishes to pound and grind them dry. For together with their drying out, the pomegranate rinds also have a weaker power, which is lost along with their moisture as it is dispersed. It is safer, therefore, to take them while still full of juice and boil them beforehand in wine until they become easy to grind. The wine must by all means be austere, but neither too young—for austere wines are coarse in their parts before they have aged—nor too old, so that it has already become pungent. For the young wine does not penetrate into the depth of the body, while the old is pungent and biting. Better, therefore, is the one intermediate in age. This would be, for the most part, between the five-year-old and the ten-year-old.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σῶρυ, γενναίαν

Now the drug which it is our purpose to discuss has as its most powerful ingredients sory, the split alum, 697 the pomegranate rinds and the oak-gall, which, however, do not have a vigorous discutient power, but the one called astringent. It is quite clear that the gall one should always put in is the kind called omphacitis (unripe), and the alum the Egyptian and split kind. If we lack this, [one should use] the round alum, which is of coarser parts than the split, but no less astringent. That the rinds of the pomegranate are called sidia everyone knows. Yet the period of their prime is short, if one wishes to pound and grind them dry. For as they dry, the pomegranate rinds also come to have a weaker power, since it is lost together with their moisture as that is dispersed. It is therefore safer to take them while still juicy and boil them first in wine until they become easy to grind smooth. The wine must in every case be austere, but neither very new (for austere wines are of thick parts before they have aged) nor very old, so that it is already pungent. For the new wine does not penetrate into the depth of the body, while the old is pungent and biting. Better, then, is the one intermediate in age. This would in general be one between five and ten years old.

Greek (Kühn)

698 ἀνασμνῆσαι δὲ καὶ νῦν ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστι τοῦ πολλάκις πολλοῖς εἰρημένου περὶ τῶν αὐστηρῶν καὶ στρυφνῶν καὶ ὅλως στυφόντων οἴνων τε καὶ σιτίων καὶ φαρμάκων, ἔτι δὲ πρότερον ὑπὲρ τῶν κατὰ τὰς εἰρημένας φωνὰς σημαινομένων. ἐκ μὲν γὰρ τῆς αὐστηρὸν τὸ μετρίως στῦφον σημαίνεται παρὰ τοῖς Ἕλλησιν, ἐκ δὲ τῆς στρυφνὸν τὸ βιαίως καὶ σφοδρῶς. τὸ δ’ ἀμφοῖν γένος κοινὸν ἐκ τοῦ τῆς στύψεως ὀνόματος δηλοῦται, μὴ προσδιασαφουμένου πότερα σφοδρῶς ἢ ἀμυδρῶς. πάντα μὲν οὖν τὰ στύφοντα συνάγει καὶ πυκνοῖ τοὺς πόρους κᾀπιδεῖ καὶ σφίγγει τὴν οὐσίαν τῶν ὁμιλούντων ἑαυτοῖς μορίων. ἧσσον δὲ καὶ μᾶλλον ἐργάζεται τοῦτο πρὸς τὸ ποσὸν τῆς στύψεως. ἐν δὲ τῷ σφίγγεσθαι καὶ πυκνοῦσθαι τὸ ἐπιπολῆς τοῦ στυφομένου σώματος ἀποκλείεταί τε καὶ κωλύεται πρὸς τὸ βάθος ἰέναι τῶν στυφόντων ἡ δύναμις. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καλῶς ἐπενόησαν ἔνιοι τῶν ἰατρῶν μιγνύναι τοῖς τοιούτοις φαρμάκοις ἕτερα λεπτομερῆ καὶ δριμεῖαν ἔχοντα δύναμιν ἕνεκα τοῦ δι’ αὐτῶν εἰς τὸ βάθος τοῦ σώματος ἄγεσθαι ποδηγουμένας τὰς τῶν στυφόντων δυνάμεις.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀνασμνῆσαι (read as ἀναμνῆσαι, 'to recall')

698 It is necessary now too to recall what has often been said to many people concerning austere and harsh wines, foods and drugs, and in general astringent ones, and, before that, concerning what is signified by the terms just mentioned. For by the word 'austere' is signified, among the Greeks, that which is moderately astringent, and by 'harsh' that which is violently and intensely so. The genus common to both is indicated by the name 'astringency', without its being further specified whether it is intense or faint. All astringent things, then, draw together and condense the pores, and bind and constrict the substance of the parts that come into contact with them. But they do this to a lesser or greater degree in proportion to the amount of astringency. And in the constricting and condensing of the surface of the body being astringed, the power of the astringents is shut out and prevented from proceeding to the depth. For this reason some physicians have rightly devised the mixing with such drugs of others that are fine-parted and possess a sharp power, in order that through these the powers of the astringents may be conducted, under their guidance, into the depth of the body.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀνασμνῆσαι (apparently a misprint for ἀναμνῆσαι, 'to call to mind'), ποδηγουμένας

698 It is necessary now too to call to mind what has often been said by many about austere and harsh and, in general, astringent wines, foods and drugs, and, even before that, about the things signified by the terms just mentioned. For by the term 'austere' (αὐστηρόν) the Greeks signify that which is moderately astringent, and by 'harsh' (στρυφνόν) that which is violently and intensely so. The genus common to both is indicated by the noun 'astringency' (στῦψις), when it is not further specified whether intensely or faintly. Now all astringent things draw together and condense the pores, and bind and constrict the substance of the parts with which they come into contact. They do this to a lesser or greater degree according to the quantity of the astringency. But while the surface of the body being astringed is constricted and condensed, the power of the astringent agents is shut out and prevented from going into the depth. And for this reason some of the physicians have well devised to mix with such drugs others that are fine-particled and have a pungent power, so that through them the powers of the astringent agents may be conducted into the depth of the body, guided along the way.

Greek (Kühn)

τοῦτ’ οὖν αὐτὸ νοήσας ὁ συνθεὶς αὐτὸ τὸ 699 προκείμενον φάρμακον ἔμιξεν αὐτῷ τὴν τίτανον ἄσβεστον. ἐν δὲ τῇ τοῦ Ἀνδρομάχου βίβλῳ παραλέλειπται μὲν ἡ τῆς τιτάνου προσηγορία, τὸ δὲ τῆς ἀσβέστου μόνον ὄνομα γέγραπται, πολλὰ καὶ ἄλλα τῶν ἰατρῶν ἕνεκα συντόμου λέξεως οὕτως ἑρμηνευόν των. αὐτίκα χοῦν ἐν αὐτοῖς τοῖς φαρμάκοις, ὅσα πρὸς δυσεπούλωτα καὶ κακοήθη συντεθείκασι, λεπίδος ἁπλῶς γεγραμμένης χωρὶς τοῦ προσκεῖσθαι χαλκοῦ καὶ σχιστῆς χωρὶς στυπτηρίας καὶ τὸ τερμινθίνης χωρὶς τοῦ προσκεῖσθαι τὸ ῥητίνης· προσυπακούομεν αὐτοῖς τὰ παραλελειμμένα κατὰ τὴν λέξιν οὕτως, ὥσπερ καὶ νῦν ἐπὶ τῆς ἀσβέστου τὴν τίτανον. ὀνομάζουσι γὰρ κατ’ ἐξοχὴν τὴν αὐτὴν μόνην ἐσβεσμένην τε καὶ ἄσβεστον, οὐχ ἧττον καὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις ὅσα καίεται, πρεπούσης τῆς προσηγορίας. μίξας οὖν τὴν ἄσβεστον τίτανον ὁ συνθεὶς τὴν ἔμπλαστρον τήνδε τοῖς στρυφνοῖς φαρμάκοις, ἰσχυρὸν εἰργάσατο τὸ ἐξ ἁπάντων σύνθετον ἕνεκα τοῦ μέχρι τοῦ βάθους ἀφικνεῖσθαι τὴν δύναμιν αὐτοῦ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χοῦν

Having understood this very point, then, the one who composed the 699 drug before us mixed unslaked lime into it. In the book of Andromachus, however, the designation 'lime' has been omitted, and only the word 'unslaked' is written, physicians expressing many other things too in this way for the sake of brevity of expression. Thus, to take an immediate example, in these very drugs which they have composed for ulcers hard to cicatrize and malignant ones, when 'scale' is written simply, without 'of copper' being added, and 'split' without 'alum', and 'terebinth' without 'resin' being added, we supply in thought for them the words omitted in the expression in this way, just as now too, in the case of 'unslaked', we supply 'lime'. For they call this alone, par excellence, both 'slaked' and 'unslaked', although the designation befits no less all the other things that are burned. So the one who composed this plaster, by mixing unslaked lime with the harshly astringent drugs, made the compound of all of them strong, so that its power reaches as far as the depth.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χοῦν (read as γοῦν, 'for instance'), ἑρμηνευόν των (read as ἑρμηνευόντων), τὴν αὐτὴν μόνην

Having grasped this very point, then, the one who composed the 699 drug under discussion mixed unslaked lime (titanos) into it. In the book of Andromachus the designation 'lime' (titanos) has been left out, and only the word 'unslaked' (asbestos) is written, since physicians express many other things in this way too for the sake of concise wording. For instance, in the very drugs which they have composed for hard-to-cicatrise and malignant [sores], 'scale' (lepis) is written simply, without 'of copper' being added, and 'split' (schistē) without 'alum', and 'terebinth' without 'resin' being added. We supply mentally for them the words left out of the expression in this way, just as now, in the case of 'unslaked', we supply 'lime'. For they call this substance alone, par excellence, 'slaked' and 'unslaked', although the designation fits no less the other things that are burned. Having mixed the unslaked lime, then, into the harshly astringent (stryphna) drugs, the one who composed this plaster made the compound of all of them strong, so that its power reaches as far as the depth.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But since the composition had come to be made from the strongest drugs, and contained nothing assuaging or mitigating, nor 700 anything of the concocting material, he reasonably mixed into it a pus-producing drug, frankincense. Reasonably, then, the mixing of such drugs has not been the same for all who have composed such plasters. It is clear, then, that those before 702 me who composed such drugs also differed among themselves for the reasons stated above. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὅπως αὐτὸ παραδιδόναι ποιοῦσι, ἐξινίσωσι, αὐτοφυὲς

And you must also bear in mind, in the composition, this point too, which is neglected by most physicians and in which some even go wrong through ignorance. 703 For they trust the dealers in petty wares who profess to supply old oil, not knowing how they produce what they hand over: they melt old pig's fat, once they have thoroughly stripped it of its fibres, and mix it with common oil. For in this way it appears thick to those who look at it and foul-smelling to those who smell it, like the old oil. The foul smell, then, is similar, but to one who remembers each of the two precisely the difference is clear. For the peculiar character of the smell of fat is one thing, that of old oil another. And the thickness of the adulterated oil itself is not continuous nor homoeomerous, as is that of the unadulterated, but it also becomes thicker toward the bottom of the vessel, while what is on the surface is thinner; which is why those who have counterfeited it also stir it up when giving it to the buyers. But even if the oil is truly genuine, without any such knavery, it has indeed this name 'old', but its potency is not close—that of the three-year-old compared with the thirty-year-old, or that of the four-year-old compared with the fifty-year-old. For both oil and fat acquire in addition a certain bitingness with time, when they have grown old.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐξινίσωσι, ῥωποπώλαις, διακινοῦσιν

You must also remember the following in compounding, a point neglected by most physicians and by some even 703 got wrong through ignorance. For they trust the petty-ware dealers who profess to supply old oil, not knowing how they produce what they hand over: they take old pig's fat, once they have thoroughly stripped it of its fibres, melt it, and mix it with common oil. Made in this way it looks thick to those who see it and smells foul to those who smell it, like the old oil. The foul smell is indeed similar, but to someone who accurately remembers each of the two the difference is clear. For the peculiar quality of the smell of fat is one thing, and that of old oil another. Moreover, the thickness of the adulterated product itself is not continuous, nor homoeomerous, as is that of the unadulterated oil; rather, it becomes thicker at the bottom of the vessel, while the part on the surface is thinner. That is why those who have counterfeited it stir it up when they hand it to the buyers. But even if the oil is truly natural, without such knavery, it bears this very name 'old', yet its potency is not close: the three-year-old is not near the thirty-year-old, nor the four-year-old the fifty-year-old. For both the oil and the fat acquire a certain biting quality over time, when they have grown old.

ἀλλὰ αὕτη μὲν μεγίστη, 704 μεγίστη δὲ ἡ κατὰ δύναμιν ἐξαλλαγή. διαφορικώτατα γάρ ἐστι τό τε ἔλαιον καὶ τὸ στέαρ, ὅταν παλαιωθῇ. καὶ τούτου πειραθεὶς ἐγὼ διὰ τὸ κατά τινα τύχην ἔλαιόν τε καὶ στέαρ ἔχειν παλαιότατον, τοῦ πατρὸς ἐκ πολλοῦ φυλάττοντος αὐτὰ, αὐτὸς πάλιν ἄμφω κατέθηκα δαψιλῆ, κομιδῇ νέος ὢν, ὅπως ἔχοιμι χρονίζουσιν αὐτοῖς χρῆσθαι μετὰ τὸ καταναλῶσαι τὰ τοῦ πατρός. ἀλλὰ καὶ μετὰ ταῦτα πάλιν οὐκ ὀλιγάκις ἀπεθέμην ἀμφότερα παλαιωθησόμενα. καὶ νῦν ἐστί μοι τοσοῦτον πλῆθος αὐτῶν, ὡς ἐξαρκέσαι κᾂν ἑκατὸν ἔτη ζήσω. καὶ τοίνυν τὸ προκείμενον ἐν τῷ λόγῳ φάρμακον ἔλαιόν τε καὶ στέαρ ἐτῶν τεσσαράκοντα λαβὸν ἀεὶ παρ’ ἐμοὶ τὰ κακοηθέστατα τῶν πολυχρονίων ἑλκῶν ὠφέλησε. ἐλπίδος μειζόνως. ἔστι δὲ τὰ τοιαῦτα τῶν ἑλκῶν, ἐφ’ ὧν αὐτὸ τὸ πεπονθὸς μόριον ἐν καχεξίᾳ τοσαύτῃ γέγονεν, ὡς κᾂν εὔπεπτον ᾖ τὸ παραγιγνόμενον ἐπ’ αὐτὸ τροφῆς ἕνεκεν αἷμα, μεταβάλλειν τε καὶ διαφθείρειν, ἐάν τε κακόχυμον ᾖ τὸ ἐπιῤῥέον εἰς τοσοῦτον, ὡς εἰ καὶ μηδὲν ἐπεπόνθει τὸ μόριον, ὑπ’ ἐκείνου γοῦν αὐτοῦ μόνου διαβιβρώσκεσθαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: αὕτη μὲν μεγίστη, μεγίστη δὲ

But this is very great, 704 and greatest is the alteration in respect of power. For both oil and fat become most discutient when they have aged. And having had experience of this, because by some chance I had very old oil and fat, my father having kept them for a long time, I myself in turn laid down both in abundance, when I was quite young, so that I might be able to use them as they grew old, after having used up my father's. But also after this I again, not seldom, stored away both to be aged. And now I have so great a quantity of them as to suffice even if I live a hundred years. And so the drug under discussion, having received oil and fat of forty years, has always in my hands benefited the most malignant of long-standing ulcers, beyond expectation. Such are those ulcers in which the affected part itself has come into so great a cachexia that, even if the blood arriving at it for the sake of nourishment is well-concocted, it alters and corrupts it; or in which what flows in is cacochymous to such a degree that, even if the part had suffered nothing, it would be eaten away by that alone.

But this is very great, and very great too is the alteration in potency. For both oil and fat become most strongly discutient when they have been aged. Having put this to the test, since by some chance I had very old oil and fat, my father having kept them for a long time, I myself in turn, while still quite young, laid up both in abundance, so that I might be able to use them aged after I had used up my father's. And after this too I again stored away both, not infrequently, to be aged. And now I have so great a quantity of them that it would suffice even if I were to live a hundred years. And so the drug under discussion, having always received with me oil and fat forty years old, benefited the most malignant of chronic ulcers beyond expectation. Such ulcers are those in which the affected part itself has come into so great a cachexia that, even if the blood arriving at it for the sake of nourishment is well concocted, it alters and corrupts it; and if what flows in is cacochymous to such a degree that, even if the part had suffered nothing at all, it would be eaten through by that alone.

705 θαυμαστῶς οὖν ὁ συνθεὶς αὐτὸ τὰ μὲν στυπτικὰ φάρμακα δύναμιν ἀποκρουστικὴν ἔχοντα παρέλαβεν, ὡς μηδὲν ἐπιῤῥέοι τῷ πεπονθότι μορίῳ κατὰ τὸν τῆς ἰάσεως καιρὸν, τὰ δὲ διαφορητικὰ, χάριν τοῦ τοὺς περιεχομένους ἰχῶρας ἐν τῷ πεπονθότι μορίῳ διαφορῆσαί τε καὶ κενῶσαι. περὶ δὲ τῆς συμμετρίας αὐτῶν ᾧδε ἔχει. τῆς μὲν σχιστῆς στυπτηρίας καὶ τῶν σιδίων, ὡς σφοδρότατα στυφόντων, ἔλαττον ἐνέβαλεν, ἑκκαίδεκα δραχμαῖς ἑκατέρου μίξας εἰς ταὐτὸ, τοῦ σώρεως δὲ τὸ ἡμιόλιον ἑκατέρου, τουτέστι τέσσαρας ἐπὶ ταῖς εἴκοσι, ἧττον μὲν στύφοντος, οὐχ ἧττον δὲ ξηραίνοντος, ἴσον δὲ συναμφοτέρου, τῆς τε σχιστῆς καὶ τῶν σιδίων, ἐνέβαλλε τῆς κηκί δος ἐπὶ ταῖς τριάκοντα δραχμαῖς δύο προσθεὶς, ὡς ἀσθροισθῆναι τὰς πάσας ἐκ τῶν φαρμάκων ὀκτὼ καὶ ὀγδοήκοντα. τούτοις δ’ ἀντέταξεν ἰσχυρῶς διαφορητικόν τε καὶ ξηραντικὸν φάρμακον τὴν ἄσβεστον, ἀρκεσθεὶς ἑκκαίδεκα δραχμαῖς, ἱκαναὶ γὰρ αὗται τὴν τῶν στυφόντων ποδηγῆσαι δύναμιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀσθροισθῆναι, κηκί δος, ποδηγῆσαι

705 Admirably, then, the one who composed it took on the one hand the astringent drugs, which possess a repelling power, so that nothing should flow to the affected part during the time of the cure, and on the other hand the discutient ones, for the sake of dispersing and evacuating the ichors contained in the affected part. As for their proportion, it stands thus. Of the split alum and the pomegranate rinds, since they are most vehemently astringent, he put in less, mixing sixteen drachms of each together into the same; but of sory half as much again as of each of these, that is twenty-four, since it is less astringent but no less drying; and an amount equal to both of them together, the split alum and the pomegranate rinds, he put in of oak-gall, adding two to the thirty drachms, so that the total from the drugs came to eighty-eight. Against these he set a strongly discutient and drying drug, quicklime, contenting himself with sixteen drachms, for these suffice to guide the power of the astringents.

At any rate, since one hundred and four drachmas had been gathered together from the metallic drugs, it would have been possible 706 to mix even more frankincense with them; and the drug would have become milder by just so much as more had been mixed in. But wishing to keep it strong, he mixed in less. You, however, may compound it in two ways. For after you have levigated the first five and mixed them all together, divide the resulting aggregate into two portions, and with the one portion mix sixteen drachmas of frankincense, with the other double that; then, having melted and cooled and scraped the cerate that results from the meltable drugs, and having divided this too in two, mix the one part with the one containing the greater amount of frankincense, and the other with the remaining one. And as regards the proportion of wax and fat, the first conjecture has sufficient latitude, since we do not know precisely what sort of thing the drug will be in its consistency. But after everything has been mixed it becomes clear whether it has turned out softer or harder than is proper, so that in the second composition we approach the conjecture more precisely, both of these very things and, along with them, of the oil.

Greek (Kühn)

ὥσπερ γὰρ ἄνευ λογικῆς μεθόδου συνθεῖναι φάρμακον ἀδύνατόν ἐστιν, οὕτως ἄνευ πείρας οὐχ ﻿ 707 οἷόν τε γνῶναι βεβαίως τε καὶ ἀκριβῶς τὴν δύναμιν αὐτοῦ. τοῦτο μὲν οὖν τὸ φάρμακον αὐτὸς ὁ Ἀνδρόμαχος ἔφη τοὺς ἀπεγνωσμένους ἀρήγειν. ἐφεξῆς δ’ αὐτοῦ πρὸς παρωνυχίας τι γράψας, μετ’ αὐτὸ πάλιν οὕτως ἔγραψεν αὐτοῖς ὀνόμασι. πρὸς τὰ κακοήθη ἐπὶ Βαλλερίας Σεκούνδης. προσυπακοῦσαι δὲ δεῖ δηλονότι τὸ ἐχρησάμην. ἡ δ’ ὕλη τοῦ φαρμάκου δηλοῖ θεραπείας μὲν ἀγωνιστικῶς ἀπολειπομένη, παρηγορεῖν δὲ δυναμένη τὰ παροξυνόμενα διὰ τῆς τῶν ἀγωνιστικῶν φαρμάκων προσαγωγῆς. ἡ οὖν σύνθεσις αὐτοῦ τοιάδε τίς ἐστι. ♃ Τυῤῥηνικοῦ δραχ. η΄. προσυπακοῦσαι δὲ δηλονότι τοῦ κηροῦ, στέατος ταυρείου δραχ. δ΄. τερμινθίνης δραχ. δ΄. μολύβδου κεκαυμένου δραχ. δ΄. ἐμμότῳ χρῶ. ταῦτα μὲν ἔγραψεν αὐτῇ λέξει. κακῶς δὲ ἐποίησε μήτε πρὸς τίνα κακοήθη φησὶν ἁρμόττειν αὐτὸ δηλώσας, μήθ’ ὡς ἀγωνιστικὸν, μήθ’ ὡς παρηγορικόν ἐστι προσθείς. ἕτερα γάρ ἐστι παρηγορικὰ φάρμακα, τὰ μὲν καὶ προσωφελοῦντα βραχύ τι τὰς διαθέσεις, ἔνια δὲ καὶ βλάπτοντα βραχύ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀγωνιστικῶς, ἐμμότῳ

For just as it is impossible to compound a drug without rational method, so without experience it is not 707 possible to know its power securely and accurately. Now this drug, Andromachus himself said, brings aid to those who have been despaired of. Next after it, having written something for paronychiae, after that he wrote again as follows, in these very words: "For malignant ulcers, in the case of Valeria Secunda." One must obviously supply in addition "I used." The material of the drug makes clear that it falls short of a treatment of the decisive (agonistic) sort, but that it is able to soothe the parts that have been exacerbated by the application of the decisive (agonistic) drugs. Its composition, then, is roughly as follows. Take of Tyrrhenian, 8 drachms — one must obviously understand "wax" — of bull's suet, 4 drachms; of terebinth, 4 drachms; of burnt lead, 4 drachms. Use it on a lint pledget (emmoton). This is what he wrote, in his very words. But he did badly in neither indicating for which malignant ulcers he says it is suitable, nor adding whether it is decisive (agonistic) or soothing. For soothing drugs are a different class: some of them also benefit the conditions a little, and some even harm them a little.

This is what he wrote, word for word. But he did badly in neither making clear for which malignant [conditions] he says it is suitable, nor adding whether it is combative or soothing. For soothing drugs are of different kinds: some also benefit the conditions slightly besides, but some even harm them slightly.

and some neither benefiting nor harming, but only soothing the pains; about these it will be spoken again 708, and many such remedies, put to the test through long experience, will be written down. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μαλθακῶδες, γο

of red scale, 24 drachms; 709 of ladanum, 9 ounces; of litharge, 3 pounds; of oil, 1½ xestai; of good wine, 1½ xestai. This plaster I too have prepared, having approved its material, since it contains nothing of a soft nature (μαλθακῶδες). For this is how Hippocrates names the whole class of drugs that possess an innate moisture, such as fat is, and all the oily drugs, and obviously oil itself and water. Resin and wax too have something of the soft in them. In the middle between them—I mean between those that are dry in their mixture and the soft ones—stands pitch. And for this reason it is mixed with both classes without harm, just as litharge is too, and all the other drugs that lie about the middle mixture, even if at times they deviate slightly toward either one of the two excesses.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: γο, S", μαλθακῶδες

of red [copper] scale 709 24 drachms; of ladanum 9 ounces; of litharge 3 pounds; of oil 1½ sextarii; of good wine 1½ sextarii. This plaster I too have prepared, having approved its materials, since it has nothing emollient in it. For that is what Hippocrates calls the whole class of drugs that have an innate moisture, such as fat, and all the oily drugs, and obviously oil itself and water. Resin and wax also have something emollient about them. Midway between them, I mean between those that are dry in temperament and the emollient ones, is pitch. For this reason it is mixed harmlessly with both classes, as is litharge too, and whatever other drugs lie about the middle temperament, even if at times they deviate slightly towards either of the excesses.

τῶν τοιούτων ἐστὶ καὶ ἡ πίττα, κατά γε τὴν τοῦ ξηραίνειν καὶ ὑγραίνειν ἀντίθεσιν, ἀποκεχωρηκυῖα σαφῶς ἤδη τῶν μέσων, κατὰ τὴν ἑτέραν ἀντίθεσιν τῶν θερμαινόντων καὶ ψυχόντων φαρμάκων οὐκ ἔτι· θερμαίνει γὰρ μετρίως καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ τοῖς ἐκπυΐσκουσι φαρμάκοις μίγνυται, ὥστε κατὰ τὸ προκείμενον φάρμακον ἡ μὲν ξηρὰ 710 πίττα τῶν μέσων ἐστὶ καὶ λιθάργυρος ἔτι μᾶλλον, τά τ’ ἄλλα τῶν ξηραντικῶν. δυναμένης δὲ κᾀνταῦθα παρακοῆς γενέσθαι διαλέξομαι περὶ αὐτῆς ἀναμνήσας γε πρῶτον εἰς πολλὰ χρησίμου θεωρήματος, ὃ καὶ κατ’ ἄλλας μὲν εἴρηταί μοι πραγματείας, ἱκανῶς δ’ ἐξείργασται κατά γε τὴν περὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων καὶ τὴν τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου. θερμὸν γὰρ καὶ ψυχρὸν, ὑγρόν τε καὶ ξηρὸν ὀνομάζομεν φάρμακον, ὅ τι περ ἀνθρώπου σῶμα θερ μαίνειν ἢ ψύχειν ἢ ξηραίνειν ἢ ὑγραίνειν δύνηται, πρὸς τὴν ἀρίστην κρᾶσιν ἐν ἀνθρώπῳ τὴν ἀναφορὰν ποιούμενοι. μέση δέ τις αὕτη τῶν μεμπτῶν, ἃς καὶ δι’ αὐτὸ τοῦτο θερμὰς ἢ ψυχρὰς ἢ ὑγρὰς ἢ ξηρὰς ὀνομάζομεν, ἀπὸ τῆς ἐπικρατούσης ἐν αὐταῖς εἴτε ποιότητος εἴτε δυνάμεως εἴτ’ οὐσίας εἴτε κράσεως ἐθέλοις φάναι παρονομάζοντες. κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν λόγον οὐδ’ εἰ παρώνυμον ἀπὸ τοῦ πλεονάζοντος ἐν τῇ μίξει στοιχείου τὴν ὅλην κρᾶσιν ἐθέλοις λέγειν, ἐμποδίσεις τῇ τῶν πραγμάτων ἐπιστήμῃ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τά τ’ ἄλλα τῶν ξηραντικῶν

Pitch too is among such things, at least with respect to the opposition of drying and moistening, since it has already clearly departed from the intermediate drugs, but with respect to the other opposition, that of heating and cooling drugs, not yet; for it heats moderately, and for this reason it is also mixed with the drugs that bring things to suppuration, so that in the drug under discussion the dry 710 pitch is among the intermediate ones, and litharge still more so, and the others are among the drying drugs. But since a misunderstanding can arise here too, I shall discuss it, having first recalled a theorem useful for many purposes, which has been stated by me in other treatises as well, but has been sufficiently worked out in the treatise on simple drugs and in that of the therapeutic method. For we call a drug hot and cold, moist and dry, whichever is able to heat or cool or dry or moisten the human body, making our reference to the best temperament in a human being. And this is a kind of mean among the blameworthy temperaments, which for this very reason we call hot or cold or moist or dry, naming them derivatively from what predominates in them, whether you wish to call it quality or power or substance or temperament. And by the same reasoning, not even if you should wish to call the whole temperament by a name derived from the element that is in excess in the mixture will you impede the scientific knowledge of the matters.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀποκεχωρηκυῖα σαφῶς ἤδη τῶν μέσων, οὐκ ἔτι, παρακοῆς

Pitch too is among such substances, at least with respect to the opposition between drying and moistening, though it has already clearly departed from the middle ones; with respect to the other opposition, that between heating and cooling drugs, it is no longer of this kind. For it heats moderately, and for this reason it is also mixed into drugs that bring to suppuration. So in the drug before us the dry 710 pitch is among the middle substances, and litharge still more so, as are the other drying substances. Since a misapprehension can arise here too, I shall discuss it, but first I shall recall a theorem useful for many purposes. I have stated it in other treatises as well, but it has been worked out sufficiently in the treatise On Simple Drugs and in that of the Therapeutic Method. We call a drug hot or cold, moist or dry, whatever is able to heat or cool or dry or moisten the body of a human being, making the reference to the best mixture in a human being. This mixture is a kind of middle among the faulty ones. For this very reason we call those hot or cold or moist or dry, naming them derivatively from whatever predominates in them, whether you wish to call it a quality or a power or a substance or a mixture. By the same reasoning, if you wish to speak of the whole mixture derivatively from the element that is in excess in the mixing, you will not hinder the knowledge of the matters themselves either.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὀχθῶδες

For it has been shown in the therapeutic treatise that the discovery of remedies comes about through indication, 711 from the affections themselves, of the mixture contrary to them, and from the nature of each animal as a whole, and again in turn of each individual part, of the mixture that is proper and similar to it; so that even if someone has a simple hollow ulcer, the treatment will come about from drugs that are the same in kind, but which have no small difference in their particular species. And for this reason it was shown that, of ulcers that are indistinguishably alike, some are treated by drugs that dry sufficiently—root of panax, aristolochia, akoron and iris—while some are treated by frankincense alone, even though it does not produce flesh but rather brings to suppuration those natures in which aristolochia produces flesh. And just as in the bodies being treated the difference in particular cases is very great, so it is in the same way with ulcers. For a simple ulcer that is hollow and clean is treated by drugs that dry moderately, whereas one that is foul and malignant, raised like a bank at its lips but flabby in the flesh beneath the skin, requires drugs that dry most vehemently.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀχθῶδες, διαπυΐσκοντος, τῇ κατὰ τὸ δέρμα σαρκὶ

For it has been shown in the therapeutic treatise that the discovery of remedies comes about through indication: 711 from the affections themselves comes the indication of the mixture contrary to them, and from the nature of each animal as a whole, and again of each individual part, the indication of what is proper and similar to it. So even if someone has a simple hollow ulcer, the treatment will be carried out with things that are the same in genus but differ considerably in their particular species. And for this reason it was shown that, among ulcers which are indistinguishably alike, some are cured by strongly drying drugs (root of panax, birthwort, acorus and iris), and some by frankincense alone, although it does not fill with flesh but rather brings to suppuration those natures which birthwort fills with flesh. And just as among the bodies being treated the particular difference is very great, so in the same way it is with ulcers. For a simple ulcer, hollow and clean, is treated by moderately drying drugs, but one that is foul and malignant, ridged at the lips and flabby in the flesh along the skin, requires the most vehemently drying drugs.

ἐπὶ τούτων οὖν τῶν ἑλκῶν, ἐπειδὴ καὶ περὶ τούτων ὁ λόγος ἐστὶν, ὁ μὲν λιβανωτὸς ﻿ 712 οὕτως ἐστὶν ἀσθενὲς φάρμακον, ὡς μηδὲ διαπυΐσκειν δύνασθαι. λιθάργυρος δὲ τούτου μὲν ἰσχυρότερον, ἀλλ’ ἔτι καὶ αὐτὸ κατωτέρω τῶν μέσων. ἡ ξηρὰ δὲ πίττα τῶν μέσων, ὥσπερ γε ταύτην ὑπεράνω κατὰ τὸ ξηραίνειν ὁ κεκαυμένος χαλκὸς ἥ τε χρυσοκόλλα καὶ τὸ διφρυγές. ἐναργῶς δὲ ξηραίνει χαλκῖτις κεκαυμένη καὶ ἰὸς καὶ μίσυ καὶ χάλκανθος ἥ τε λεπὶς τοῦ χαλκοῦ, καὶ κατὰ θάτερον τρόπον ὅσα στρυφνὰ, κηκὶς, ὀμφακῖτις, σίδια, στυπτηρία. διαβρωτικὰ δὲ πρὶν καυθῆναι χαλκῖτίς τε καὶ ἰὸς καὶ μίσυ καὶ τίτανος. ὅσα μὲν οὖν ἰσχυροτάτας ἔχει δυνάμεις, ἐπὶ πάντων δοκεῖ τῶν σωμάτων τὴν αὐτὴν ἐνέργειαν ἐπιδείκνυσθαι· τὰ δὲ ἀσθενῆ σαφῆ τὴν διαφορὰν ἐνδείκνυται, προσέχοντί σοι δὲ τὸν νοῦν οὐδὲ ἧττον φανεῖται. καὶ τοῖς ἰσχυροῖς φαρμάκοις οὐ μικρὰ διαφορὰ τῆς ἐνεργείας, ἣν ποιεῖται κατὰ τὰ μαλακὰ καὶ σκληρὰ σώματα, καὶ γὰρ θᾶττον ἅπτεται τῶν μαλακῶν καὶ σφοδρότερον ἀνιᾷ καὶ φλεγμονὰς ἐπεγείρει μείζονας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

In the case of these ulcers, then, since the discussion concerns these as well, frankincense 712 is so weak a drug that it cannot even bring about suppuration. Litharge is stronger than this, but it too is still below the middle ones. Dry pitch is among the middle ones, just as burnt copper, chrysocolla and diphryges are above it in respect of drying. Burnt chalkitis, verdigris, misy, chalcanthum and scale of copper dry manifestly, and, in the other manner, all the astringent substances: oak-gall, omphakitis, pomegranate rinds, alum. Before being burnt, chalkitis, verdigris, misy and quicklime are corrosive. Now those which have the strongest powers seem to display the same activity upon all bodies, whereas the weak ones show the difference clearly; but if you pay attention there will appear no less, in the strong drugs too, a considerable difference in the activity which they produce upon soft and upon hard bodies; for they take hold of soft bodies more quickly, distress them more violently, and arouse greater inflammations.

In the case of these ulcers, then, since it is about these too that our discussion is concerned, frankincense 712 is so weak a drug as not even to be able to bring about suppuration. Litharge is stronger than this, but it too is still below the middle ones. Dry pitch is among the middle ones, just as above it in drying stand burnt copper, chrysocolla and diphryges. Manifestly drying are burnt chalcitis, verdigris, misy, chalcanthum and flake of copper, and, in the other manner, the astringent things: oak-gall, omphacitis, pomegranate rinds, alum. Erosive before they are burnt are chalcitis, verdigris, misy and lime. Now, those that have the strongest powers seem to display the same action on all bodies, whereas the weak ones show the difference clearly; yet if you pay attention it will appear no less: even in the strong drugs there is no small difference in the action they exert on soft and on hard bodies, for they lay hold of the soft ones more quickly, cause more violent pain, and arouse greater inflammations.

Greek (Kühn)

ὁπότ’ οὖν ταῦτά μοι χρησίμως οὐκ εἰς τὰ παρόντα μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ εἰς ἄλλα πολλὰ τῶν κατὰ τήνδε τὴν πραγματείαν 713 εἴρηται, πάλιν ἐπὶ τὸ προκείμενον ἀφίξομαι φάρμακον, ἐφ’ οὗ τῆς μὲν ξηρᾶς πίττης ἔφην ὑπὸ τοῦ Κρίτωνος γεγράφθαι δραχ. ρ΄. λιθαργύρου δὲ λίτρας γ΄. οὐδετέρου μὲν αὐτῶν ἐκ τῆς οἰκείας δυνάμεως ἰᾶσθαι δυναμένου κακόηθες ἕλκος, ἵνα δ’ ἔμπλαστον γένηται τὸ συντιθέμενον φάρμακον, εὐλόγως μιχθέντων τοῖς ὠφελοῦσιν. ἡ δὲ ποσότης αὐτῶν στοχασμῷ τεχνικῷ ληφθεῖσα κατὰ τὸν εἰρημένον ἀρτίως ἐγένετο σταθμὸν, εὐλαβουμένου τοῦ συντεθέντος ἢ οὕτως ὀλίγον ἑκατέρου μῖξαι τοῖς ἰσχυροῖς φαρμάκοις, ὡς τὰ δακνώδη τε καὶ διαβρωτικὰ λυπηρὰ γενέσθαι τοῖς θεραπευομένοις, ἢ οὕτω πολλὰ κατά τε τὸν ὄγκον καὶ τὸν σταθμὸν, ὡς ἐκλῦσαι τὴν δύναμιν αὐτῶν ἥ τε γὰρ τοῦ χαλκοῦ λεπὶς ἰσχυρῶς ξηραίνει τε καὶ ῥύπτει καὶ μᾶλλον ἔτι ταύτης ὁ ἰός. ἄμφω δὲ ταῦτα δρᾷ καὶ διαβιβρώσκει τὰς σαρκάς. ὁ δὲ κεκαυμένος χαλκὸς οὔτ’ ἐπιτείνεσθαι πρός τινος οὔτ’ ἐκλύεσθαι χρῄζει, σύμμετρος ὑπάρχων κατὰ δύναμιν εἰς τὰ παρόντα. πρόδηλον δ’ ὅτι τοῦ μὲν ἐλαίου τὸ μέτρον ἔκ τε τῶν μιγνυμένων αὐτῷ καὶ τῆς κατὰ τὴν ἕψησιν ἐστοχάσθη ποσότητος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συντεθέντος, ἄμφω δὲ ταῦτα δρᾷ

Since, then, these things have been said by me usefully not only for the present matters but also for many others in this treatise, 713 I shall return again to the drug under discussion, in which I said that 100 drachms of dry pitch had been written by Criton, and 3 litrae of litharge—neither of them being able, from its own proper power, to heal a malignant ulcer, but having reasonably been mixed with the beneficial ingredients so that the drug being compounded might become a plaster. Their quantity, taken by a conjecture grounded in art, came out at the weight mentioned just now, the compounder taking care neither to mix so little of each with the strong drugs that the biting and corrosive ones became painful to those being treated, nor so much in both bulk and weight as to dissolve their power; for the scale of copper dries and cleanses strongly, and verdigris still more than it, and it does both these things and also eats through the flesh. Burnt copper, however, needs neither to be intensified by anything nor to be relaxed, being of moderate power for the present purposes. And it is evident that the measure of the oil was estimated both from the things mixed with it and from the quantity of the boiling.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συντεθέντος, ἔμπλαστον, ἐκλῦσαι

Since, then, I have said these things usefully not only for the present matters but also for many others in this treatise, 713 I shall return again to the drug under discussion. For it, I said, Crito had written 100 drachms of dry pitch and 3 litrai of litharge. Neither of them is able by its own proper power to heal a malignant ulcer, but they were reasonably mixed with the beneficial ingredients so that the drug being compounded might become emplastic. Their quantity, arrived at by technical estimation, came to the weight just stated. The compounder took care neither to mix so little of each with the strong drugs that the biting and erosive ones would become painful to those being treated, nor so much in both bulk and weight as to slacken their power. For scale of copper dries and cleanses strongly, and verdigris still more than it; and both, besides doing these things, also eat through the flesh. Burnt copper, however, needs neither to be intensified nor to be slackened by anything, being moderate in power for the present purposes. And it is plain that the measure of the oil was estimated from the things mixed with it and from the amount of boiling.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπὶ πλέον γὰρ ἑψηθῆναι δεῖται 714 τὰ μεταλλικὰ παρασκευαζόμενα πρὸς τὸ ξηραίνειν ἀδήκτως. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ τὴν ἕψησιν αὐτοῖς προσέθηκεν ὁ Κρίτων ᾧδέ πως γράψας αὐτοῖς ὀνόμασιν. ἕψε λιθάργυρον, ἔλαιον ἕως συστραφῇ ποσῶς, εἶτε λεπίδα χαλκοῦ καὶ ἰὸν ἕως συστραφῇ καὶ εὔχρους γένηται, εἶτα πίσσαν ἐπίβαλλε, εἶτα ψύξας λάδανον λεῖον γενόμενον σὺν οἴνῳ προσέχων μὴ ὑπερζέσῃ τούτου βληθέντος, εἶτα ἐπὶ μαλακοῦ πυρὸς ἑνώσας κατάχει καὶ μάλασσε. αὕτη μὲν ἡ τῆς ἑψήσεως γραφή. ἐγχωρεῖ δὲ καὶ πλεῖον ἔλαιον βαλόντας ἐπὶ πλέον ἑψῆσαι, πρῶτον μὲν τὴν λιθάργυρον, εἶτα κατὰ ταύτην ἐμβληθέντας τόν τε ἰὸν καὶ τὴν λεπίδα καὶ τὸν χαλκόν. τὰ γὰρ ἐπὶ πλέον ἑψόμενα διττὴν ἐπί γε τῶν τοιούτων ἑλκῶν ἀρετὴν ἐπικτᾶται, ξηραντικώτερα ταὶ ἀδηκτότερα γινόμενα. περὶ μὲν οὖν τῶν ἄλλων φαρμάκων τῶν ἁπλῶν, ὅσα κατὰ τὴν προκειμένην ἔμπλαστρον εἴρηται τῷ Κρίτωνι, λέλεκταί μοι συμμέτρως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: εἶτε, ταὶ

For the metallic substances, when they are being prepared so as to dry without biting, require to be boiled for a longer time. 714 And for this reason Criton also added the boiling for them, writing somewhat as follows, in these very words: boil litharge and oil until it thickens to some degree, then the scale of copper and the verdigris until it thickens and becomes of good colour, then add the pitch, then, having cooled it, the ladanum made smooth with wine, taking care that it does not boil over when this is thrown in; then, having united them over a gentle fire, pour it out and knead it. This, then, is the written prescription for the boiling. But it is also possible, having added more oil, to boil for a longer time, first the litharge, and then the verdigris, the scale and the copper, thrown in upon it. For things boiled for a longer time acquire a twofold virtue, at least in the case of ulcers of this kind, becoming more drying and less biting. Concerning the other simple drugs, then, all those that are mentioned by Criton in the plaster before us, I have spoken in due measure.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: εἶτε (read as εἶτα, 'then'), συστραφῇ ποσῶς ('thickens somewhat'), κατὰ ταύτην ἐμβληθέντας (passive participle where active ἐμβαλόντας is expected; rendered 'throwing in upon it'), ταὶ (read as τε καὶ), μάλασσε ('knead', or possibly 'soften')

For metallic substances that are being prepared so as to dry without biting need to be boiled for a longer time 714. And for this reason Crito also added the boiling for them, writing roughly as follows, in these very words: 'Boil litharge and oil until it thickens somewhat; then scale of copper and verdigris, until it thickens and becomes of good colour; then throw in the pitch; then, having cooled it, add ladanum made smooth with wine, taking care that it does not boil over once this has been thrown in; then, having united it over a gentle fire, pour it out and knead it.' This, then, is the written prescription for the boiling. It is also possible to put in more oil and to boil for a longer time: first the litharge, then, throwing in upon it the verdigris, the scale and the copper. For things that are boiled for a longer time acquire a twofold virtue, at any rate in ulcers of this kind, becoming both more drying and less biting. Concerning the other simple drugs, then, all those named by Crito in the plaster under discussion, I have said as much as is appropriate.

ἐφύλαξα δὲ τὸν περὶ τοῦ λαδάνου λόγον, ὅτι καὶ μόνον ἁρμόττει τοῖς δυσεπουλώτοις σὺν οἴνῳ τριβόμενον, οὐχ ὅτι δεῖται οἴνου δυνάμεως, ἀλλ’ ὅτι σκληρότερόν 715 ἐστιν ἢ κατὰ τὴν ἔμπλαστρον. ἀλλ’ ἐάν τις αὐτῷ μίξας ἔλαιον ἑψήσῃ, τὴν δύναμιν ἀμβλύνει τοῦ φαρμάκου. ἔστι δ’ οὐχ οὕτως ἰσχυρὸν ὡς ἀμβλυνθῆναι δεῖσθαι· καθάπερ ὁ ἰὸς ἢ ἡ χαλκῖτις ἢ ἡ λεπὶς ἤ τι τοιοῦτον καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μαλακώτερον. ἔστι δὲ τοιοῦτον τὸ νέον, ἐπιτηδειότατον γάρ ἐστιν εἰς ἐπούλωσιν ἑλκῶν δυσεπουλώτων. ἐν Κύπρῳ γοῦν ἔνθα πλεῖστον γίγνεται, πρόσφατον ἔτι καὶ μαλακὸν ὑπάρχον αὐτὸ μαλάττων τις ἰατρὸς ἐπετίθει τοῖς τοιούτοις ἕλκεσιν· εἰ δὲ καὶ βραχὺ σκληρυνθείη, κατὰ μυρσίνου τὴν μάλαξιν ἐποιεῖτο, μὴ παρόντος δὲ μυρσίνου, μετὰ κυπρίνου ἢ ῥοδίνου ἕτερον δὲ φάρμακον οὐ μετὰ πολὺ τοῦδε κατὰ τὴν τετάρτην βίβλον ὁ Κρίτων ἔγραψεν ἐν αὐτοῖς ὀνόμασιν ᾧδε. ἡ διὰ χαμαιλέοντος ποιοῦσα πρὸς τὰ αὐτὰ τῇ Τυρίᾳ. ἔστι δὲ ἐπουλωτικωτάτη τῶν δυσαλθῶν καὶ τυλωδῶν καὶ χρονίων ἑλκῶν. ἰᾶται δὲ καὶ τὰς ἐν τῷ δακτυλίῳ ῥαγάδας καὶ κονδυλώματα καὶ σκληρίας. λιθαργύρου μνᾶ α΄. κηροῦ μνᾶς S". ἰοῦ μνᾶς τέταρτον καὶ χαμαιλέοντος ὄγδοον, ἐλαίου μνᾶ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μαλακώτερον, ἔστι δὲ τοιοῦτον τὸ νέον

But I have reserved the account of ladanum, namely that even on its own it suits ulcers hard to cicatrize when triturated with wine—not because it needs the power of wine, but because it is harder 715 than is suited to the plaster. But if one mixes oil with it and boils it, one blunts the power of the drug. And it is not so strong as to need to be blunted, as verdigris or chalcitis or scale or any such thing is, and for this reason it is softer. Such is the fresh ladanum, for it is most suitable for the cicatrization of ulcers hard to cicatrize. In Cyprus, at any rate, where it is produced in the greatest quantity, a certain physician, kneading it while it was still fresh and soft, applied it to such ulcers; and if it had hardened even a little, he carried out the softening with myrtle-oil, and when myrtle-oil was not available, with henna oil or rose-oil. Another drug, not long after this one, Criton wrote down in the fourth book in these very words, as follows. The plaster through chamaeleon, effective for the same things as the Tyrian. It is the most cicatrizing of ulcers hard to heal, callous and chronic. It also cures fissures in the anus and condylomata and indurations. Of litharge, 1 mina; of wax, half a mina; of verdigris, a quarter of a mina, and of chamaeleon an eighth; of oil, a mina.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μαλακώτερον, σκληρότερόν ἐστιν ἢ κατὰ τὴν ἔμπλαστρον, κατὰ μυρσίνου, μνᾶς S"

I have reserved the account of ladanum, because even by itself it suits hard-to-cicatrise sores when triturated with wine. This is not because it needs the power of wine, but because it is harder 715 than is right for the plaster. But if one mixes oil with it and boils it, one blunts the power of the drug. Yet it is not so strong as to need blunting, as verdigris or chalcitis or copper flake or some such thing does; and for this reason it is milder. Such is the fresh ladanum, for it is most suitable for the cicatrization of hard-to-cicatrise sores. In Cyprus, at any rate, where most of it is produced, a certain physician used to work it soft while it was still fresh and soft and apply it to such sores. If it had hardened even a little, he carried out the softening with myrtle-oil, and when myrtle-oil was not to hand, with oil of cypros or rose-oil. Another drug, not long after this one, in the fourth book, Criton wrote out in these very words, as follows: The [plaster] made with chamaeleon, effective for the same things as the Tyrian. It is most cicatrizing of intractable, callous and chronic sores. It also heals fissures in the anus, condylomata and indurations. Litharge, 1 mina; wax, ½ mina; verdigris, a quarter of a mina, and chamaeleon, an eighth; oil, a mina.

Greek (Kühn)

ἔλαιον καὶ λιθάργυρον ἕψε κινῶν 716 σπάθῃ, ἕως ὅσου συστῇ καὶ γένηται μηλίνη. τούτου δὲ γινομένου ἰὸν ἐπίπασον. ἔπειτα πρόσβαλλε κηρὸν, τήξας ἐπιμελῶς καὶ ἄρας ἀπὸ τοῦ πυρὸς ἐπίπασον τὸν χαμαιλέοντα, ὅταν δὲ ἑνωθῇ, κατέρα εἰς θυείαν καὶ μαλάξας ἀναιροῦ. τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον ἀντὶ μὲν τοῦ λαδάνου τὸν χαμαιλέοντα τῆς αὐτῆς ὄντα δυνάμεως ἔλαβεν. ἀμφότερα γάρ ἐστιν ἐπουλωτικὰ τῶν δυσιάτων ἑλκῶν. ἡ διαφορὰ δ’ αὐτῶν ἐν τῷ τὸν μὲν κεκαυμένον χαλκὸν ἔχειν ἐκεῖνο καὶ μηδ’ ὅλως ἔχειν τὸν κηρὸν, ἐν τούτῳ δὲ τῷ τὸν μὲν κηρὸν εἶναι, τὸν χαλκὸν δὲ οὐκ εἶναι, καὶ τοῦτ’ εὐλόγως ἐποίησεν. ἐν ἐκείνῳ μὲν γὰρ ἀντὶ τοῦ κηροῦ τῷ λαδάνῳ συνεχρήσατο πρὸς τὴν τῶν ἀνίκμων φαρμάκων ἕνωσιν, ἐνταυθοῖ δὲ μηδὲν ἔχων τοιοῦτον τὸν κηρὸν ἔμιξε, μαλακτικὸν μὲν ὄντα μετρίως φάρμακον, οὐ μὴν ἐπουλωτικόν γε. δυνατὸν δ’ ἦν κᾀνταῦθα πλέον ἔλαιον ἐμβεβλῆσθαι, καθάπερ γε καὶ παλαιὸν ἢ κίκινον. ἐγὼ δὲ εἰς τὰ τοιαῦτα καὶ σχίνινον ἐμβάλλω πλεόνων μετέχον δυνάμεων. καὶ γὰρ μαλακτικὸν ἔχει τι καὶ διαφορητικὸν καὶ στυπτικὸν, ὧν ἁπάντων δεῖται τὰ μετὰ σκληρότητος τῶν χειλῶν δυσεπούλωτα ἕλκη.

Boil the oil and the litharge, stirring 716 with a spatula, until it thickens and takes on the colour of quince-yellow. When this has happened, sprinkle in the verdigris. Then add the wax, and having melted it carefully and taken it off the fire, sprinkle in the chamaeleon; when it has been united, pour it out into a mortar and, having kneaded it, take it up. This drug took chamaeleon, which is of the same power, in place of the ladanum; for both are cicatrizing for hard-to-heal ulcers. Their difference lies in the fact that that one contains burnt copper and contains no wax at all, whereas in this one there is wax, but no copper; and he did this reasonably. For in that one he made use of the ladanum, in place of wax, for the uniting of the moistureless drugs, whereas here, having nothing of that kind, he mixed in wax, which is a moderately emollient drug, but certainly not a cicatrizing one. It would have been possible here too for more oil to have been put in, as also old oil or castor oil. I myself, for such cases, also put in mastic oil, which partakes of several powers; for it has something emollient and discutient and astringent, all of which are needed by ulcers hard to cicatrize that are accompanied by hardness of the lips.

Boil the oil and the litharge, stirring 716 with a spatula, until it thickens and becomes quince-yellow. When this has happened, sprinkle on the verdigris. Then add the wax, having melted it carefully, and, lifting it from the fire, sprinkle on the chamaeleon; and when it has become united, pour it out into a mortar and, having softened it by kneading, take it up. This drug took chamaeleon in place of ladanum, since it is of the same power, for both are cicatrizing of hard-to-heal sores. The difference between them lies in this: the former has burnt copper and has no wax at all, whereas in this one there is wax but there is no copper. And he did this reasonably. For in the former he used ladanum instead of wax for the uniting of the drugs that lack moisture, whereas here, having nothing of that sort, he mixed in wax, which is a moderately emollient drug, though certainly not a cicatrizing one. It would have been possible here too to put in more oil, as indeed also old oil or castor oil. I, for my part, also put mastic-oil into such preparations, since it shares in several powers. For it has something emollient, something discutient and something astringent, all of which are required by hard-to-cicatrise sores accompanied by hardness of the lips.

717 [Περὶ τῶν ὑφ’ Ἡρακλείδου γεγραμμένων.] Καιρὸς οὖν ἤδη καὶ τῶν ὑφ’ Ἡρακλείδου τοῦ Ταραντίνου γεγραμμένων ἐν τῇ πρὸς Ἀστυδάμαντα βίβλῳ μνημονεῦσαι. πιστὸς γὰρ ἀνὴρ εἴπερ τις ἄλλος ἐστὶ, τὰ διὰ τῆς πείρας αὐτῷ κεκριμένα μόνα γράφων. οὗτος οὖν ὁ ἀνὴρ κατὰ λέξιν οὕτως ἔγραψε. πρὸς τὰ παλαιὰ ἕλκη. λεπίδος μέρος ἓν, ἰοῦ ξυστοῦ μέρη δ΄. κηρωτῇ μυρσίνῃ προσαναλαμβάνεται ὀκταπλῇ. ὁ μὲν οὖν Ἡρακλείδης, ὡς ἂν ἰατροῖς γράφων ὑπομνήματα, προσέγραψε, κηρωτῇ μυρσίνῃ ἀναλάμβανε. τολμηροὶ δ’ οὕτως εἰσὶ πολλοὶ τῶν νῦν, ὡς πρὸ τοῦ παρὰ διδασκάλων κτήσασθαί τινα ἕξιν ἰατρικὴν, ἐκ τῶν τοιούτων βιβλίων ὁρμώμενοι χρῆσθαι καλοῖς φαρμάκοις ἀμεθόδως καὶ διὰ τοῦτο πολλάκις ἀποτυγχάνειν. αὐτίκα γέ τοι τῆς μυρσίνης κηρωτῆς ἔξεστιν ἔλαττόν τε καὶ πλέον μίξαντα ποτὲ μὲν ὑγροτέρῳ τε καὶ μαλακωτέρῳ χρῆσθαι τῷ φαρμάκῳ, ποτὲ δὲ σκληροτέρῳ καὶ ξηροτέρῳ, κατὰ μὲν τὴν πρώτην ἐπίθεσιν ἐν πλατεῖ στοχασμῷ, καθάπερ εἴρηται πρόσθεν, ἐν δὲ τῇ δευτέρᾳ χρήσει πλησιαίτερον ἀφικνουμένου, ὡς ἐδείχθη.

717 [On what was written by Heraclides.] It is now time, then, to mention also what was written by Heraclides of Tarentum in his book addressed to Astydamas. For he is a trustworthy man if anyone is, writing only those things that have been judged by him through experience. This man, then, wrote word for word as follows: For old ulcers. Of scale, one part; of scraped verdigris, four parts. It is taken up with eightfold myrtle cerate. Now Heraclides, as one writing memoranda for physicians, added: take it up with myrtle cerate. But many of the physicians of today are so bold that, before acquiring from teachers any medical competence, they set out from such books to use good drugs unmethodically, and for this reason often fail. For example, it is possible, by mixing in less or more of the myrtle cerate, at one time to use the drug moister and softer, at another harder and drier—at the first application by a broad conjecture, as was said before, but in the second use arriving closer to the mark, as was shown.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πλησιαίτερον, ἀφικνουμένου, προσαναλαμβάνεται, ἕξιν

717 [On the remedies written by Heracleides.] It is time, then, now to make mention also of the remedies written by Heracleides of Tarentum in his book addressed to Astydamas. For he is a trustworthy man, if ever anyone was, since he writes only what has been judged by him through experience. This man, then, wrote word for word as follows: 'For old ulcers. Of scale one part, of scraped verdigris four parts. It is taken up in addition with an eightfold quantity of myrtle cerate.' Now Heracleides, as one writing notes for physicians, added 'take up in myrtle cerate'. But many of those of the present day are so rash that, before acquiring from teachers some medical proficiency, they set out from books of this kind to use fine drugs without method, and for this reason often fail. For instance, by mixing in less or more of the myrtle cerate, one may at one time use the drug moister and softer, at another harder and drier: at the first application by a broad conjecture, as was said earlier, but in the second use coming nearer to the mark, as was shown.

Greek (Kühn)

718 βλέποντες γὰρ τὸ ἕλκος, εἰ μὲν ἐνδεῶς ἐξηράνθη, σφοδρότερον ἐργασόμεθα τὸ φάρμακον, ὡς μᾶλλον ξηραίνειν· εἰ δ’ ἐπὶ πλέον ἢ δεῖ, μαλακώτερον αὐτὸ ποιήσομεν πλείονα μίξαντες τὴν μυρσίνην κηρωτήν. ἡ γὰρ τῶν μεταλλικῶν δύναμις οὐ μόνον ὑπὸ ταύτης, ἀλλὰ καὶ πάσης κηρωτῆς ἐκλύεται. ἑξῆς δὲ τούτῳ προσγράψας, ἄλλη· προσυπακοῦσαι δὲ χρὴ δηλονότι δύναμις· ὑπέταξε ταυτί. λεπίδα λεάνας ἐπίχεε ὄξος καὶ τρῖβε ὑπὸ κύνα, μέλιτος ποιῶν πάχος ἢ ἕψε. ὅταν δὲ ξηρὸν γένηται, προσεπιλεάνας μίσγε κηρωτῆς μέρη ὀκτὼ καὶ χρῶ πρός τε τὰ πρόσφατα τραύματα καὶ τὰ παλαιὰ ἕλκη καὶ σύρματα καὶ τρίμματα καὶ χίμεθλα τὰ μὴ ἄγαν ἑλκώδη. γινώσκειν δ’ ὑμᾶς χρὴ καὶ περὶ τοῦδε τοῦ φαρμάκου ταῦτα. πρὸς μὲν τὰ ἄλλα μετρίως ἄν τις ὀκταπλασίαν κηρωτὴν μίξειε τῇ λεπίδι, πρὸς δὲ τὰ παλαιὰ πάνυ, τουτέστι τὰ δυσίατα, κᾂν ἑξαπλασίαν ἢ πενταπλασίαν μίξῃς, οὐ βλάψεις, ἰσχυροτέρων γὰρ δέονται. τὴν δὲ περὶ τοῦ φαρμάκου διήγησιν ἔγραψεν ὁ Ἡρακλείδης οὕτως. ἐγχωρεῖ δὲ καὶ ἐπὶ πυρὸς ἀναξηράναντα τὴν λεπίδα χρῆσθαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: σύρματα, τρίμματα, ὑπὸ κύνα

718 For, looking at the ulcer, if it has been insufficiently dried, we shall make the drug more vigorous, so that it dries more; but if it has been dried more than it should, we shall make it softer by mixing in more of the myrtle cerate. For the power of the metallic substances is relaxed not only by this one but by every cerate. Next after this, having added the word "another"—one must obviously understand "power" in addition—he appended the following: Having ground scale of copper fine, pour vinegar over it and triturate it under the Dog-star, making it the thickness of honey, or boil it. And when it has become dry, grind it further and mix in eight parts of cerate, and use it for fresh wounds and old ulcers and abrasions and chafings and chilblains that are not excessively ulcerous. You must know the following also concerning this drug: for the other conditions one may, with moderation, mix eightfold cerate with the scale; but for the very old ones, that is the hard-to-heal ones, even if you mix in sixfold or fivefold, you will do no harm, for they need stronger ones. Heracleides wrote the account of the drug thus: it is also possible to use the scale after having dried it out over a fire.

718 For, looking at the ulcer, if it has been dried insufficiently, we shall make the drug more vigorous, so that it dries more; but if it has been dried more than it should, we shall make it milder by mixing in more of the myrtle cerate. For the potency of metallic substances is relaxed not only by this but by every cerate. Next after this he added the heading 'Another', and one must of course understand 'potency' along with it, and set down the following beneath it: 'Grind the scale fine, pour vinegar over it and triturate it under the Dog-star, bringing it to the thickness of honey, or boil it. For the other conditions one would do well to mix eightfold cerate with the scale; but for very old ones, that is, the hard-to-heal ones, you will do no harm even if you mix sixfold or fivefold, for they need stronger remedies. Heracleides wrote the account of the drug as follows: 'It is also possible to use the scale after drying it over a fire.'

βούλεται 719 δ’ ὁ λόγος αὐτῷ, κᾂν ἐν ἄλλῳ τινὶ καιρῷ συντιθῶμεν τὸ φάρμακον, ἀντὶ τῆς ἡλιακῆς θερμασίας χρῆσθαι πυρί. καλλίω μέντοι γίγνεται τὰ τοιαῦτα πάντα κατὰ τὸν θερινὸν ἥλιον ἐν πολλαῖς ἡμέραις λειούμενα, τὰ μὲν δι’ οἴνου, τὰ δὲ δι’ ὄξους. λεπτομερέστερα μὲν οὖν γίγνεται τὰ δι’ ὄξους λειωθέντα καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καθαιρετικώτερά τε καὶ λεπτυντικώτερα καὶ ξηραντικώτερα. πρὸς δὲ τὰς ἐπουλώσεις τὰ δι’ οἴνου βελτίω, καὶ χρεία γε τούτων γίγνεται προξηρανθέντων, εἶτα προκαθαρθέντων τῶν δυσεπουλώτων ἑλκῶν. προξηρανθήσεται μὲν οὖν διὰ τῶν ἰσχυρῶς ξηραινόντων, καθαρθήσεται δὲ διὰ τῶν ῥυπτόντων, ἀπολωθήσεται δὲ διὰ τῶν στυφόντων. ἄλλο. ἐφεξῆς δ’ ἕτερον φάρμακον ἔγραψεν ὁ Ἡρακλείδης ἐκ μὲν τῆς αὐτῆς ὕλης, οὐκ ἰσχυρὸν δὲ διὰ τὸ πολὺ τῆς λιθαργύρου μῖξαι τῷ διφρυγεῖ. τῆς μὲν γὰρ λιθαργύρου πέντε καὶ τριάκοντα δραχμὰς ἀξιοῖ βάλλειν εἰς αὐτὸ, τοῦ διφρυγοῦς δὲ ὀκτώ. καὶ ταῦτα δὲ ἀναλαμβάνει κηρωτῆ, τοῦ μὲν κηροῦ ἐχούσης δραχ. μ΄. τοῦ δ’ ἐλαίου κο. γ΄.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀπολωθήσεται

His statement 719 means that, even if we compound the drug at some other season, we may use fire in place of the heat of the sun. All such preparations, however, turn out better when triturated over many days in the summer sun, some with wine, others with vinegar. Those triturated with vinegar, then, become finer in their parts, and for this reason more reducing, more attenuating and more drying. For cicatrization, however, those triturated with wine are better, and need of these arises when ulcers hard to cicatrize have first been dried and then first been cleansed. They will be dried beforehand, then, by means of the strongly drying agents, cleansed by means of the detergent agents, and cicatrized (ἀπολωθήσεται) by means of the astringent ones. Another. Next Heraclides wrote another drug, made from the same material but not strong, because he mixed a large quantity of litharge with the diphryges. For he directs that thirty-five drachms of litharge be put into it, but eight of diphryges. And these he takes up with a cerate containing forty drachms of wax and three cotylae of oil.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀπολωθήσεται (apparently a corruption of ἀπουλωθήσεται; translated 'cicatrised'), καθαιρετικώτερα (rendered 'more reductive', i.e. removing excess flesh; could be 'more cleansing'), κο. γ΄ (abbreviation taken as κοτύλας, 'cotylae')

His 719 meaning is that, even if we compound the drug at some other season, we should use fire in place of the sun's heat. All such preparations, however, turn out finer when they are triturated in the summer sun over many days, some with wine, others with vinegar. Those triturated with vinegar become finer in their parts, and for this reason more reductive, more attenuating and more drying. For cicatrisations, however, those made with wine are better, and there is need of them once the hard-to-cicatrise sores have first been dried and then first cleansed. They will be dried beforehand by means of things that dry strongly, cleansed by means of detergent things, and cicatrised by means of astringent things. Another. Next Heraclides wrote another drug from the same material, but not a strong one, because he mixed much litharge with the diphryges. For he directs that thirty-five drachms of litharge be put into it, and eight of diphryges. And these too he takes up in a cerate containing 40 drachms of wax and 3 cotylae of oil.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χειρώνεια, ἀμμωνιακοῦ θυμιάματος

I myself have often used diphryges alone with such a 720 cerate — not, however, even this for phagedaenic, Chironian and putrefactive ulcers, for it falls far short in power of the drugs that treat such conditions, but in cases of ulcers difficult to cicatrize that are without great malignancy; so that if one also mixes litharge with it, and that in large quantity, he will make the drug weak. After this there is written, next in order, a drug of the same profession, as follows. Take wax, 12 drachmas; terebinth resin, 3 drachmas; scale, 4 drachmas; ladanum, 4 drachmas; asphalt, 3 drachmas. Grind the ladanum separately, and the scale; then melt the others with myrtle oil and mix. But if the ladanum is soft, melt it. In this recipe Heracleides clearly indicated that, when the ladanum is hard, one should triturate it by itself in the mortar, but when it is moist and soft, one should melt it over a fire, either alone or with a little oil — which you have seen me always doing, not only with this, but also with ammoniac incense. The drug written after this by the Tarentine is also of the same profession, in the following proportion. Take chalcitis, 25 drachmas.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χειρώνεια, λεπίδος, ἐπαγγελίας, ἀμμωνιακοῦ θυμιάματος

I myself have often used diphryges alone with a cerate of this kind 720, though not even it against phagedaenic, chironian and putrefying ulcers, for it falls far short in power of the remedies that treat such conditions, but rather for ulcers that are hard to cicatrise without great malignancy. Hence if someone also mixes litharge with it, and that in large quantity, he will make the drug weak. After this, a drug of the following kind is recorded as belonging to the same declared purpose. Take: wax, 12 dr.; terebinth resin, 3 dr.; scale, 4 dr.; ladanum, 4 dr.; asphalt, 3 dr. Grind the ladanum by itself, and the scale as well; then melt the other ingredients with myrtle oil and mix. If the ladanum is soft, melt it. In this prescription Heraclides has clearly indicated that when the ladanum is hard one should grind it by itself in the mortar, but when it is moist and soft one should melt it down over the fire, either alone or with a little oil. You have seen me do this constantly, not only with this substance but also with ammoniac incense. The drug written after this one by the Tarentine is also of the same declared purpose, in the following proportion. Take: chalcitis, 25 dr.

721 of cadmia, 12½ drachmas. Having ground them separately and having mixed in unadulterated wine (ἀπαράχυτος) and boiled it, pour it over, so that the mass is moistened in due measure; then grind until it is dried, and having melted 50 drachmas of wax, 30 drachmas of terebinth resin, 8 cotylae of myrtle oil, mix 20 drachmas of the dry ingredients with the cerate. And this drug too contains none of the emollient ingredients. In its mixture with the cerate, however, the man trained rationally in the methods I have laid down would suitably work out the quantity of the mixture appropriate for each of the ulcers. And rightly, too, he pours the unadulterated wine—that is, one with which seawater has not been mixed—hot over the chalcitis and the cadmia, wishing to remove the acridity of the chalcitis by means of its heat. Another noteworthy drug is written continuously after this one, composed of simples of the following number and kind: of scale of stomoma (hardened iron), 4 drachmas; of scraped verdigris, 2 drachmas—in some of the copies 1 drachma is written; of chrysocolla, 25 or 15 drachmas; of wax, 25 drachmas. In some copies, however, we found neither the wax nor the verdigris.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: λίαν

Having ground the other ingredients fine, 722 melt the wax with a little resin, so that the drug is not excessively hard, and having mixed them, use it; and on top apply a sponge wrung out of vinegar. Demetrius the Bithynian said that he used the chrysocolla itself, taking it up in very hot wax. Heraclides indicated in the case of this drug too that the resin is mixed into such drugs not for a primary use, but in order to supply them with a certain viscosity toward a plaster-like consistency, and especially when the wax is neither oily by nature nor fresh, but hard and parched. The scale of steel (stomōma), however, dries strongly, in addition to having also an astringent power. And he said that Demetrius the Bithynian used it in this way, taking up the chrysocolla alone in the wax. And Demetrius did well to use it thus; just as I too often use, in such cases, not only chrysocolla but also each of the others by itself, softening it with soft wax.

It is clear that by 'each of the others' I meant those which I went through earlier as benefiting such ulcers, and that I have such drugs levigated, compounded with wax, for 723 each of the hard-to-heal ulcers that I may be treating. For sometimes I take up chrysocolla in wax, sometimes burnt copper or scale—and this either of stomoma or of iron or of copper—sometimes verdigris, unburnt or burnt, as also misy and chalcitis. And sometimes I also mix in one of the austere substances, about which I spoke earlier, whenever I wish to repel that which flows in; among these are alum, pomegranate rinds, oak-gall, the cytini and balaustion, and all other such things. Trusting me, then, that none of all the drugs mentioned up to this point is to be rejected, practise the method of their use, so that you may succeed just as I do. And since two other noteworthy drugs were written next in order by the Tarentine, it seemed reasonable to me to add those as well. The first of them he wrote roughly thus: Take scale, 4 drachms; manna, 4 drachms; split alum, 2 drachms; misy, 2 drachms; having triturated them with vinegar and made little pastilles weighing 2 drachms each, store away.

It is clear that I said that each of the others which I went through earlier benefits such ulcers, and that I grind them, compounding such drugs with the wax, for each 723 of the intractable ulcers that I treat. Rx: of scale, 4 dr.; of manna, 4 dr.; of split alum, 2 dr.; of misy, 2 dr. Having ground them with vinegar and made pastilles weighing 2 dr. each, store them away.

Greek (Kühn)

ὑπὸ δὲ τὴν χρείαν κηροῦ 724 δραχ. δ΄. ἄλλ. μ΄. ῥητίνης πιτυΐνης δραχ. β΄. στέατος ταυρείου δραχ. δ΄. τήξας καὶ ἐάσας παγῆναι μίσγε τῷ τροχίσκῳ. χρῶ δὲ πρὸς τραύματα ὡς κολλώδει καὶ ἀφλεγμάντῳ. ταῦτα μὲν ὁ Ταραντῖνος. ἐγὼ δὲ τῇ μὲν οὕτως μιχθείσῃ φησὶ χρῆσθαι δέον πρὸς τραύματα, πρὸς μέντοι τὰ κακοήθη τῶν ἑλκῶν οὐ χρὴ μιγνύειν τὴν τοιαύτην κηρωτὴν, ἀλλ’ ἱκανὸς ὁ κηρὸς μόνος, ὡς ἐπὶ τῶν ἔμπροσθεν εἴπομεν. καὶ εἰ δεήσει ποτὲ μῖξαί τι τῶν μαλθακωδῶν αὐτῷ, βραχὺ μύρσινον ἢ κύπρινον ἢ ῥόδινον ἔστω. βλάψεις δ’ οὐδὲν, οὐδ’ εἰ τὴν λάρικα προσαγορευομένην ῥητίνην μίξειας, οὐδὲ τὴν τερμινθίνην, ἧττον μὲν ἐπιτήδειον ὡς πρὸς τὰ τοιαῦτα τῶν ἑλκῶν οὖσαν, οὐ μὴν ἀπόβλητόν γε. τὰς δ’ ἄλλας ῥητίνας οὐ χρὴ μιγνύναι ἄνευ μεγάλης ἀπορίας τῶν προειρημένων. οὕτω δὲ καὶ περὶ στέατος. οὐ γὰρ οὖν οὐδὲ τοῦτο μιγνύναι χρὴ, πλὴν εἰ καὶ τὰ χείλη μαλάττειν ἐθέλῃς. ὅτι δὲ ξηραντικώτερον ὑπάρχει τὸ ταύρειον στέαρ πάντων τῶν ἄλλων, ὅσα τῶν ἡμέρων ζώων ἐστὶν, ἀκηκόατε πολλάκις.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: φησὶ, ἄλλ. μ΄.

At the time of use, having melted wax 724 4 drachms (others: 40), pine resin 2 drachms, bull's fat 4 drachms, and having let it congeal, mix it with the troche. Use it for wounds as an agglutinating and inflammation-preventing remedy. So much for the Tarentine. For my part, [I say that] one must use it, mixed in this way, for wounds, as he says (φησὶ); for the malignant ulcers, however, one should not mix in such a cerate, but the wax alone is sufficient, as we said in the case of the preceding ones. And if it should ever be necessary to mix with it some one of the emollient things, let it be a little myrtle-oil or henna oil or rose-oil. You will do no harm either, not even if you should mix in the resin called larch, nor the terebinth, which is less suitable for such ulcers, yet not to be rejected. The other resins, however, one should not mix in except in a great lack of the aforementioned. And so too concerning fat. For indeed this too should not be mixed in, unless you also wish to soften the lips. And that bull's fat is more drying than all the others that come from domesticated animals, you have heard many times.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἄλλ. μ΄., ἐγὼ δὲ τῇ μὲν οὕτως μιχθείσῃ φησὶ χρῆσθαι δέον, φησὶ, λάρικα

At the time of use, take of wax 724 4 dr. (others: 40), pine resin 2 dr., bull's fat 4 dr.; melt them, let them set, and mix with the pastille. Use it for wounds, as it is agglutinative and anti-inflammatory. So much for the Tarentine. But I [say]: the preparation mixed in this way, he says, one ought to use for wounds; for malignant ulcers, however, one should not mix in a cerate of this kind, but the wax alone is sufficient, as we said in the case of the preceding drugs. And if it should ever be necessary to mix one of the emollient substances with it, let it be a little myrtle-oil or oil of cypros or rose-oil. Nor will you do any harm if you mix in the resin called larix, or the terebinth, which is less suitable for ulcers of this kind, yet certainly not to be rejected. The other resins, however, one should not mix in, unless there is a great lack of those mentioned before. So too with fat: one should not mix this in either, unless you also wish to soften the lips of the ulcer. That bull's fat is more drying than all the other fats that come from domestic animals, you have often heard.

For the fat of the lion, and that of leopards, 725 and obviously also that of the hyena, surpass it in fineness of parts. It is better, therefore, when fat must be mixed in, to put in goose fat, and preferably that of free-ranging geese; for it is sufficiently fine in its parts and soothing. And since there still remains one drug written down, continuous with the one written before, at which Heracleides made the beginning of his teaching, speaking thus: "the ipoterion (press-remedy), and especially for those of delicate skin" — since it belongs to the other material and power, which we said suits those who do not bear the application of strong drugs but are bitten, irritated and afflicted with flux, let the discussion concerning this be deferred, to be stated when I go through such drugs. But now I shall pass over to the drugs similar to those written before, adding only this much more about them: that Heracleides also, in another book entitled The Soldier (Stratiotes; printed στατιώτην), wrote the same drugs, disagreeing with himself only in the one that is third among them in order. For here it is written simply "of wax 40 drachms," whereas in The Soldier there is added "but some, 16 drachms"; and this is indeed better.

Greek (Kühn)

726 ἔστιν. ἡ γὰρ πολλὴ μίξις τοῦ κηροῦ καθαιρεῖ τὴν δύναμιν τοῦ φαρμάκου. τῶν αὐτῶν τούτων φαρμάκων ὁ Ἡρακλείδης μέμνηται κᾀν τοῖς πρὸς τὴν Ἀντιοχίδα γεγραμμένοις αὐτῷ, καὶ πρώτως γε τοῦ πρώτου, καθάπερ ἐν τούτοις καὶ ἐν ἐκείνοις γράφων οὕτως. πρὸς μὲν τὰ ἄγαν πεπαλαιωμένα καὶ τυλώδη χρήσῃ ταύτῃ. λεπίδος μέρος α΄. ἰοῦ κατὰ τινὰ μὲν τῶν ἀντιγράφων ἀπεστιγμένον τὸ δ΄. γέγραπται· κατὰ τινὰ δὲ γραμμὴν ἄνωθεν ἔχον μακρὰν, ὥστε ἤτοι μέρη δ΄. σημαίνειν ἢ τοῦ ἑνὸς τὸ δ΄. οὕτως δὲ καὶ κατὰ τὰ προειρημένα συγγράμματα διαφερόντως εὗρον αὐτὸ γεγραμμένον, ὥστε καὶ κατὰ τοῦτο χρεία τῆς προκειμένης πραγματείας ἐστὶν, ὅπως ἄν τις ἐπίστηται διαγιγνώσκειν τῶν φαρμάκων τὰς διαφοράς. μόνος γὰρ οὗτος αὐτοῖς κατὰ τρόπον χρήσαιτο. τοῦτο γοῦν αὐτὸ τὸ προκείμενον φάρμακον, ἐὰν μὲν τετραπλάσιον λάβῃ τὸν ἰὸν, ἱκανῶς ἔσται δριμύ. τέταρτον δὲ μέρος ἰοῦ βαλών τις ὡς πρὸς ἓν μέρος τῆς λεπίδος ἕξει μέτριον φάρμακον, ὡς πρὸς τὰ κακοήθη, καὶ μάλιστα, ἐὰν μὴ διὰ κηροῦ μόνου τὴν μίξιν αὐτῶν, ἀλλὰ διὰ κηρωτῆς ποιήσηται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀπεστιγμένον

726 is. For a large admixture of the wax diminishes the power of the drug. Heraclides mentions these same drugs also in the writings addressed by him to Antiochis, and first indeed the first of them, writing in those, just as in these, thus: "For those that are very old and callous you will use this: of scale, one part; of verdigris" — in some of the copies the δ΄ is written marked off with a point (ἀπεστιγμένον), but in some having a long line above it, so that it signifies either four parts or a fourth of one. And so too in the previously mentioned treatises I found it written differently, so that in this respect also there is need of the present treatise, in order that one may know how to distinguish the differences of the drugs. For such a person alone would use them properly. This very drug before us, at any rate, if it takes four times the amount of verdigris, will be quite pungent; but if someone puts in a fourth part of verdigris in relation to one part of the scale, he will have a moderate drug, as regards malignant [ulcers], and especially if he makes the mixture of them not with wax alone but with a cerate.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀπορῶ μὲν (probably to be read as ἀπορῶμεν)

It has been said before that the wax must be both soft and fresh; 727 but if we are at a loss for such wax, then, heating the wax we have beside a fire or in the sun and softening it with a little myrtle oil, we shall in this way mix in both the scale of copper and the verdigris. It has also been said at the beginning of this book that a most excellent drug is one composed of scale of copper and verdigris, with four times the amount of wax added to it. And for this reason I use it continually, mixing in, for the most part, also an equal amount of burnt copper. The Tarentine, however, in the book written by him to Antiochis, has added to the use of this drug things which he did not add in those earlier treatises of his that I mentioned. I shall therefore write out his whole passage, which runs as follows: "For those that are extremely inveterate and callous you will use this one: of scale of copper one part, of verdigris a fourth; having ground them fine, take them up in a hard myrtle cerate of eight times the amount and use it, having first washed the ulcer with cold water." He added, then, also that one must first wash the ulcer with cold water. Now, therefore, I shall at once write out those of the things written by Andromachus for the same professed virtue that have been passed over by me.
